## Part opening

<!-- pedagogy-unit: part:OBSERVATIONS::part-opening -->

Part ID: `part:OBSERVATIONS`

Purpose:

> Neural fluctuations reach behavior through a motor pathway, while spatial tuning describes how a position is expressed across cells. The eye and place-field studies constrain these different relationships. The final chapter connects their results without treating a static map as a complete dynamical model.

## ch:EYE-DRIFT - Why the eyes drift during fixation

Source file: `content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md`

Purpose: Looking steadily at a small spot does not hold the eye perfectly still. Between brief, rapid movements called [**microsaccades**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, the eye makes a small, smooth, irregular movement called [**fixational drift**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Does this movement begin as noise in the muscles, or does the brain send a changing command to them? Ben-Shushan and colleagues answer this in two stages. Their recordings support a large shared drive upstream of the motor neurons. Their model then proposes a particular source: random motion of the eye position stored in a memory circuit.

Prerequisites: ch:FOUNDATIONS, ch:MEMORY-DYNAMICS, ch:MEMORY-INFORMATION

Sidecar sections: From stored eye position to muscle action, What the recordings can and cannot reveal, Fitting movement to firing rate, Putting spikes in units of eye position, Why a small correlation implies shared variation, The fraction driven by the common command, Separating measurement noise from movement in the MSD, Filtering white noise and filtering Brownian motion differ, The proposed circuit from memory to movement, Which neural parameters set the amount of drift?, Reading the figures as an argument, What remains uncertain

Blocks: 0 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

> This distinction connects the previous chapters to a measurement outside the brain. A memory coordinate can diffuse inside a neural network, but the movement produced by that coordinate also depends on the muscles and the mechanics of the eye. We need to follow the signal through those stages before comparing a neural diffusion law with an eye trajectory.

### From stored eye position to muscle action

Location: line 7, anchor `sec:EYE-PATHWAY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 9
Closing passage line: 13

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 98346f6bbadeb4bd2fca3539c7b0e04f344b0708e160c2415c3cb7d790d8b5cf -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> The eye needs an ongoing command to maintain a position. A brief command to move is therefore insufficient: after the movement, a circuit must maintain activity representing the new position. The [**oculomotor integrator**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the neural circuit that performs this storage. “Integrator” refers to turning a movement command into a lasting position signal. In the proposed mechanism, the continuous attractor described in Chapters 1 and 2 stores horizontal eye position. Noise moves the stored value along that attractor.[^p2-pathway]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 326229916f9e18672b79d20aa5c7df46733a620ecb497ec93a340ebbb62f64d5 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p13

> The signal reaches [**ocular motoneurons (OMNs)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, neurons whose spikes activate the eye muscles. The recordings came from the [**abducens nucleus**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a group of brainstem neurons that includes OMNs activating the lateral rectus muscle, which turns the eye outward. Other neurons in that nucleus carry signals to other neurons rather than directly to muscle. The study separated these cell types using their eye movement response parameters. Finally, the muscles and the mechanical properties of the eye, together called the [**ocular plant**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, turn neural activity into movement.[^p2-recordings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 d97e2460fbe82bb871fc0319c264f71113bc2af4c015683911d7736f37fc7693 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p17

> There are three possible contributions to an observed drift: a changing shared command sent to many OMNs, private spike variability in individual OMNs, and variability after those neurons, including muscle and measurement noise. Private spike fluctuations can partly cancel when many neurons act together. A common command survives this averaging. This is why the noisy activity of one neuron can reveal a source that dominates the much smoother eye movement.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-RECORDINGS -->

### What the recordings can and cannot reveal

Location: line 15, anchor `sec:EYE-RECORDINGS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 17
Closing passage line: 21

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 f8a740447bda1c1c53c16adebc30af97cc2b73076a1419a10bfee56992b223b1 -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p19

> The authors reanalyzed earlier experiments in two male rhesus macaques. The animals first fixated a stationary target and then tracked a moving target. A wire search coil on one eye measured eye position while an electrode recorded one neuron's spikes. There were 57 identified OMNs. Each recording paired one OMN with eye movement; there were no simultaneous recordings of multiple OMNs. Thus the analysis cannot directly measure the covariance between two recorded motor neurons.[^p2-recordings][^p2-single]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p19 sha256 2619e69fdb09ac2d7d66b5c6495d49fb63dd6924a2b866b628207d75ff7cd44d -->
p19, lines 19-19; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p17; next: p21

> The initial stationary target was followed by 500–700 ms of required fixation before the target stepped and began moving. Neural voltage was sampled at 25 kHz and eye position at 1 kHz. The authors detected microsaccades using eye speed and acceleration, removed 30 ms on each side of them, and manually checked the remaining segments. For the main displacement analysis, segments shorter than 350 ms were excluded and longer ones were split into nonoverlapping 350 ms pieces. There were 302 such pieces per cell on average, with different counts across cells. These repeated observations improve estimates within two animals; they do not create 57 independent animal samples.[^p2-segments]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p21 sha256 c0f6b279f91fc458ea3231543ac04923d1f7f24e60c06feac718b6420164efd8 -->
p21, lines 21-21; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p19; next: p25

> The large movements have a useful role even though the scientific question concerns tiny drifts. They produce enough rate change to estimate how each cell's activity depends on eye movement. The fitted relationship is then applied during fixation. This transfer is an assumption to examine, because an incorrect [**gain**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} during drift would change the inferred common variance. Gain is the scale that converts a change in one signal into a change in the other.


Claim-bearing content: none

### Fitting movement to firing rate

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p25 sha256 2d5a4bd1401864e33fc9fc4990186090a957c2651f19cbb7b6cbd5e46eb26288 -->
p25, lines 25-25; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p32

> Position alone does not predict a motor neuron's activity during a movement. At the same position, firing can differ when the eye is moving or accelerating. The paper therefore fits

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p32 sha256 35fbef1321d69097d5535fe106bd53ca030f8ada3646621fe938d6269e80e804 -->
p32, lines 32-32; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p25; next: p40

> Here $E$ is horizontal eye position in degrees and $\widehat f$ is predicted firing rate in spikes/s. The position threshold $E_T$ sets where the position term reaches zero. The coefficients have units

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p40 sha256 c429a3f0eb82cb1acf60cb183ba8a898a8e568f9a0da15d3a0bcee8853ffa5c5 -->
p40, lines 40-40; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p32; next: p42

> Multiplying by position, velocity, and acceleration gives spikes/s in all three terms. The delay $\Delta t$ is in seconds: firing at time $t$ predicts movement at the later time $t+\Delta t$ for a positive neural lead. This is the paper's Eq. 2, including its threshold and time shift.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p42 sha256 f3fc314e12bf31a6fd59e7b34dfb99e911184c925b34e79a5945cd1023947062 -->
p42, lines 42-42; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p40; next: p49

> The observed rate $f(t)$ was estimated as the inverse of the interval between successive spikes, constant between those spikes. For each cell, the authors minimized the sum of squared rate errors,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p49 sha256 c36d17f381179b57d22c89b867c01ec25faa88683b6c1562b24179d0ee188176 -->
p49, lines 49-49; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p42; next: p51

> $a$ labels trials and $T_a$ is trial duration. This is Eq. 3 with the trial count renamed so that it cannot be confused with a neuron count. Fits used full trials, including fixation and pursuit. To predict a trial's rate, that trial was left out of the parameter fit. Acceleration was estimated from velocity after local polynomial smoothing over 21 ms. Trials in which the rate fell below 20 Hz were discarded: the linear formula is intended for active cells above firing threshold, whereas firing cannot become negative.[^p2-forward]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p51 sha256 e7c421b6dbe3a43a533b79a2e228dd1b59209fd8d1af1c8f68a7562f9624bb7c -->
p51, lines 51-51; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p49; next: p55

> During fixation, the authors subtracted each segment's mean firing rate, joined the segments, and compared observed and predicted rate fluctuations. Figure 1c illustrates a weak relationship for one cell; Fig. 1d shows a positive population effect. The one sided test asks whether the mean correlation is positive, compared with a zero-mean reference. Its [**p value**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the probability, under that reference, of a test statistic at least as large as observed. Across 57 cells it gave $p=3\times10^{-9}$. A weak single cell relationship is expected here because the tiny movement produces small rate changes against much larger spike variability. This is the first evidence of a relationship, before any estimate of the fraction of common motion.[^p2-rate-result]

### Putting spikes in units of eye position

Location: line 53, anchor `sec:EYE-INVERSE-FILTER`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 55
Closing passage line: 133

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p55 sha256 bde35c11ee6ec82d0faafab73d5dce7cf71d5e140a64ea7f6bf8e66ee4f55ace -->
p55, lines 55-55; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p51; next: p57

> To ask how much eye movement is shared with a neuron, it helps to express both signals in degrees. The paper reverses the fitted relationship: a cell's spike train becomes the input, and its prediction of eye position becomes the output. This operation is an inverse of the movement-to-rate fit, rather than a new fit to small drift residuals.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p57 sha256 4f6c7d464c5f055b961eb1cbfceab1493dc76af31b519d8f4014631f024aa40f -->
p57, lines 57-57; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p55; next: p63

> Let $x(t)=E(t)-E_T$. Ignoring the time shift for a moment, the deterministic relation is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 7f48654e5c8ea2f33c055c5899c633f4f0fe48408a259f6f7406c7737c73172a -->
p63, lines 63-63; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p57; next: p70

> A [**causal filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} computes its present output from present and past input. For the equation above, its [**impulse response**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} $h_0(u)$ is the position response to one instantaneous spike at time zero. To factor the equation, consider a time component $e^{pt}$: differentiation multiplies it by $p$. The algebraic ratio of output to input for that component is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p70 sha256 19dbd09a54232a01ba1f66b8e50cd5c1f1d56443af3ae6383d94723a2d4a446f -->
p70, lines 70-70; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p63; next: p77

> Factoring the quadratic gives the slow and fast decay times

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p77 sha256 efae1bfe1de1d6e6373840e64544427f7b1283ecf19a2cd03beaa0d935fe4881 -->
p77, lines 77-77; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p70; next: p84

> These are the paired signs in the paper's Eq. 7, with $\tau_+>\tau_-$ when $k,r,m>0$ and $r^2>4km$. In this case both components decay without oscillation. The same factorization gives two useful identities:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p84 sha256 4eec0db35e0cd5097e595acded14851f798b0df8940c381d3a89cfa31f16d34f -->
p84, lines 84-84; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p77; next: p90

> We can obtain the response to a spike directly from the differential equation. After the spike, the input is zero, and each of the two exponentials solves the remaining homogeneous equation. Write their combination as

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p90 sha256 d5a34ccb8a05bed2a1a5184b92b28398d972bd5b9f8f5e276f2e8d2167d6b11f -->
p90, lines 90-90; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p84; next: p97

> To find $A$ and $B$, treat the spike as a short pulse of unit area and shrink its duration to zero. The position change during that pulse tends to zero, so $h_0(0^+)=0$. Integrating $m h_0''+r h_0'+k h_0$ across the pulse gives $m h_0'(0^+)=1$: the velocity changes by $1/m$, while position and velocity before the causal response are zero. Therefore

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p97 sha256 a6a01a1fbe397969ebaa932d712135d84357ed5e36057e80a5ab752575837184 -->
p97, lines 97-97; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p90; next: p105

> Substituting $B=-A$ gives $A(1/\tau_- -1/\tau_+)=1/m$, hence $A=\tau_+\tau_-/[m(\tau_+-\tau_-)]$. Making the response zero before the spike gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p105 sha256 17b1baaff251def1ac150a79aebb365830c810003d113f5b384376630eed77b0 -->
p105, lines 105-105; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p97; next: p112

> $\Theta(u)$ is zero for $u<0$ and one for $u>0$; it imposes causality. Restoring the fitted neural lead delays this response: $h(u)=h_0(u-\Delta t)$. Write the spike train as $\xi(t)=\sum_j\delta(t-t_j)$, where $\delta$ is an ideal instantaneous impulse of unit area, so integration over one spike counts one event. The prediction is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p112 sha256 94fdba405561135fb2ffbc1d55ee5e10ed896152ae5250c4fac6439430ea4c04 -->
p112, lines 112-112; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p105; next: p114

> These expressions reproduce Eqs. 4–6, including the factor $\tau_+\tau_-/m$ and the delay. The [**convolution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} integral adds the responses to all past spikes. With spike count treated as a count, $\xi$ has units spikes/s and $h$ has units degrees/spike. Each spike contributes a decaying position response; adding those responses produces degrees.[^p2-inversion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p114 sha256 1682b05d154bb130d709606004cc56a1752f384ec76339a5c9586211f2f1c302 -->
p114, lines 114-114; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p112; next: p122

> The kernel's area checks its normalization:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p122 sha256 9342257b323d29a7490feb94bbe753fbac3b7da45624d5c8c5781848515e0a92 -->
p122, lines 122-122; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p114; next: p124

> Thus a constant input rate $f_0$ predicts $E_T+f_0/k$, exactly the static forward relation. A kernel with unit area would give the wrong conversion unless $k$ had already been absorbed into the input. When the two decay times coincide, $r^2=4km$, the limit is $h_0(u)=\Theta(u)u e^{-u/\tau}/m$. The distinct exponential formula is used for the fitted responses in the paper.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p124 sha256 9b2ee830691f3039bde3143aa4ee58dbc946248e991762c153bd2baf666366b1 -->
p124, lines 124-124; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p122; next: p131

> For a constructed example, take $k=4$ spikes/(s deg), $\tau_+=0.120$ s, and $\tau_-=0.005$ s. The identities above require $r=0.500$ spikes/deg and $m=0.00240$ spikes s/deg. Then

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p131 sha256 8c7bd8b13dc15819406aa2a41498f782c06fdd5848a2979e41a963c86d646976 -->
p131, lines 131-131; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p124; next: p133

> and its area is $2.174(0.120-0.005)=0.250$ deg s/spike, equal to $1/k$. A steady rate of 80 spikes/s predicts an offset of 20 degrees above $E_T$. This checks the gain as well as the time constants; it is not a fit to a recorded cell.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p133 sha256 2f42fd739920fd78587ec66c674b3c2ec94a21bf618217a41963038e35b868b0 -->
p133, lines 133-133; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p131; next: p137

> Figure 2a–b shows typical fitted times of about 5 ms and 120 ms. Figure 2c shows that a single cell's reconstructed drift is much noisier than the measured movement. Across 350 ms displacement pairs, the mean Pearson correlation was $\langle R\rangle=0.17\pm0.02$, where the error is the [**standard error of the mean (SEM)**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. SEM estimates uncertainty in the across-cell mean, rather than the spread of individual cells or uncertainty across animals. The one sided test gave $p=3.8\times10^{-12}$. Shuffling which measured segment was paired with which spike prediction centered the comparison near zero. Here and below $\langle\cdot\rangle$ over cells means a population average.[^p2-correlation]

### Why a small correlation implies shared variation

Location: line 135, anchor `sec:EYE-SHARED-VARIANCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 137
Closing passage line: 169

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p137 sha256 b54158d1b0264f8858ec0971143edd70bb5633c70c4b329e7ea088dd725c42bf -->
p137, lines 137-137; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p133; next: p139

> A correlation of 0.17 does not immediately tell us how much motion comes from the common command. First we can ask a more limited question: could all the motion come from independent motor neuron fluctuations?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p139 sha256 7b91bf7f7698525cd5c8f1eb6f2895f170f6d4d45c4d9a54f438660a098f924f -->
p139, lines 139-139; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p137; next: p147

> For a centered displacement $Y$ and one centered prediction $X$, the best linear prediction $bX$ minimizes $\operatorname{Var}(Y-bX)$. Differentiating with respect to $b$ gives $b=\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)/\operatorname{Var}(X)$. Its explained fraction of variance is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p147 sha256 5e6c5d292149e1cff11a4989c8f329d9f8f425a66076641ee95848e2644e5f6c -->
p147, lines 147-147; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p139; next: p149

> This is the [**coefficient of determination**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for a linear prediction with a fitted gain. It does not assert that the raw, unscaled position predictor itself has a small squared error.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p149 sha256 19e045d8607cf3876e73fdbde10520406a5e677e00c0c12c4b2e1857a8d6b503 -->
p149, lines 149-149; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p147; next: p157

> Suppose there are $N_m$ independent single neuron predictors $X_i$. Standardize each to variance one and also standardize $Y$. Independence makes the $X_i$ uncorrelated, so the residual after using them all has variance

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 87bd59ca3ad47cbbf9f483a691c623d8d48f9f9801046f709ee61ad1b5739a64 -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p149; next: p159

> Consequently $\sum_iR_i^2\le1$, and an average neuron explains at most $1/N_m$. This proves the bound used in Supplementary Eq. S1. It does not require equal muscle effects from all neurons. Applying it to the sampled cells does require their average explanatory power to represent the relevant motor population.[^p2-independent-bound]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p159 sha256 0514e747eaf71b383c2e16919c381e3a8ef595ae2542aeaa531c338157ab6990 -->
p159, lines 159-159; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p167

> The authors avoid directly averaging noisy squared correlations. Instead,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p167 sha256 10e160e7aaec8a9ca157723de606011692baf3f7538c100a731e753b8de2bad2 -->
p167, lines 167-167; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p159; next: p169

> Thus the estimated mean correlation implies an average explained fraction of at least about 2.9%. For 1,000 independently contributing motor neurons, the bound would be 0.1%; for several thousand it is smaller. The mismatch argues against independent OMN noise as the sole source. Shared fluctuations can allow many cells to explain the same part of the movement, so their explained fractions can overlap.[^p2-independent-bound]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p169 sha256 56567703098d6aaa1d1e145327c006422918468b8966daa0ac8fff9abcc8dcc9 -->
p169, lines 169-169; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p167; next: p173

> This step finds shared variation. Locating its source upstream also uses a biological premise: the private variability generated within OMNs is taken to be independent across cells. The paper supports that premise using anatomical and stimulation studies indicating little local recurrent coupling in the relevant motor nuclei. It was not tested by simultaneous recordings in this data set. Common inputs can correlate the total activity of OMNs even when their privately generated spike noise is independent.[^p2-independence]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-CHI -->

### The fraction driven by the common command

Location: line 171, anchor `sec:EYE-CHI`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 173
Closing passage line: 235

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p173 sha256 69163f9e82f1a289717afbced282845042a4f51f99c5a0672ec5847b693829d0 -->
p173, lines 173-173; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p169; next: p180

> The variance bound establishes that a shared signal exists, but leaves its size open. The next calculation uses the fact that the inverse filter has a calibrated gain in degrees. For one 350 ms interval, write the measured displacement and the single cell prediction as

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p180 sha256 5eaad1504420fc53de872e9e912758178739d07c311e10667820095656a8830b -->
p180, lines 180-180; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p173; next: p182

> where $C=\delta E_C$ is the common upstream contribution, $\eta$ combines motor neuron, muscle, and measurement contributions to measured displacement, and $\epsilon$ is the prediction error from the individual cell. All are displacement variables in degrees. The assumptions are that the common contribution enters both signals with unit gain, meaning the same one-for-one displacement scale, and that $C,\eta,\epsilon$ are pairwise uncorrelated to the required accuracy.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p182 sha256 d51df87be810a2b313a0e4c69cdfcce0a7d1abc6157b458b11c66767c067e344 -->
p182, lines 182-182; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p180; next: p191

> Expanding the covariance shows exactly what those assumptions buy:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p191 sha256 b72907aed662f01ddc56a1c30fde711d28bb9b2c4e3be6c23ffa0a5cc27d01fe -->
p191, lines 191-191; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p182; next: p198

> The common fraction is therefore estimated by

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p198 sha256 fa0dccc6a37f55f62728761f000ccf545242b050c2e6795366245a86f728d1eb -->
p198, lines 198-198; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p191; next: p200

> These are Eqs. 11–15 in displacement form. The gain assumption requires that the relation calibrated during larger movements still applies during drift. There is also a small covariance between $\epsilon$ and $\eta$, because the recorded neuron's private spikes contribute both to its predictor and to actual eye motion. The paper neglects this contribution as order $1/N_m$ under its many neuron model.[^p2-chi-method]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p200 sha256 01e7757b7ffe43dfc2273cd2895457e71bb691dd44694c85207a8e7d89aa11eb -->
p200, lines 200-200; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p198; next: p208

> Correlation divides the same covariance by a different denominator:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p208 sha256 1645f0bba511a3396180642788336cc4728198be9bd50a9fc44f6ecd0273e6a8 -->
p208, lines 208-208; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p200; next: p210

> Large private noise in a cell inflates $\operatorname{Var}(X)$ and makes $R$ small. It need not make the common contribution to the real movement small, because many independent private contributions average down there.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p210 sha256 1dc51e3ecb390dbfdf30bcb72a02357ed259fba0c9a4796682ab9c24ae89de9d -->
p210, lines 210-210; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p208; next: p218

> **Example: correlation 0.17 with a common fraction 0.82.** These numbers are constructed to expose the distinction, not extracted from individual cells. Take independent centered variables with

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p218 sha256 1719591b8fd5b83d7aa0be4ad31c39db34eed0e6295f26374e911180096ae5ef -->
p218, lines 218-218; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p210; next: p225

> Then $\operatorname{Var}(Y)=0.01000$, $\operatorname{Var}(X)=0.23266$, and $\operatorname{Cov}(X,Y)=0.00820$, all in deg$^2$. It follows that

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p225 sha256 340326265819a30fbab12ba07ada323a51f6ce430f6a5f60f91ca86cba3efc4f -->
p225, lines 225-225; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p218; next: p227

> One cell explains $R^2\simeq2.89\%$ of displacement variance after linear rescaling, while the common signal supplies 82% of actual displacement variance. The larger, poorly predicted part of the single cell signal is private prediction noise.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p227 sha256 a65b6fdc44c554175747a64e282a650477b18593ddf8eb1d86a93d5a0618d3a5 -->
p227, lines 227-227; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p225; next: p235

> In the experiment, each cell gives a noisy estimate $\chi_i$, and those estimates can lie outside $[0,1]$. The underlying variance fraction remains in that interval under the model, but the sample covariance need not. The paper estimates each standard error $\Delta\chi_i$ mainly from covariance uncertainty, treating the measured displacement variance in the denominator as comparatively well determined. It then uses inverse variance weighting:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p235 sha256 dce33de73aed51db8944cea730d1c0616c4da4f648d765856356d76fd5cd597b -->
p235, lines 235-235; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p227; next: p239

> To see the weighting, let normalized weights $a_i$ sum to one. Independent errors give variance $\sum_i a_i^2(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ for $\sum_i a_i\chi_i$. Minimizing this subject to $\sum_i a_i=1$ gives $a_i\propto1/(\Delta\chi_i)^2$ and the error above. The paper additionally assumes approximately normal errors around a common value. The last expression is a propagated estimation error under those assumptions. It does not measure variation across animals or uncertainty in the gain and independence assumptions. The reported result was $0.82\pm0.07$, a weighted mean and weighted error for horizontal displacement over 350 ms. It is neither a fraction of distance traveled nor the fraction that one neuron's spikes accurately predict.[^p2-chi-error]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-MEASUREMENT-NOISE -->

### Separating measurement noise from movement in the MSD

Location: line 237, anchor `sec:EYE-MEASUREMENT-NOISE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 239
Closing passage line: 263

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p239 sha256 26ce5377cb79fdb2106ca43e517c647866c0895ffa87077eaf41e22778e6fcd3 -->
p239, lines 239-239; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p235; next: p245

> The common variance calculation uses one interval length. To learn what kind of process drives motion, the authors next compare displacements at many time separations. As in Chapter 1,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p245 sha256 61e04c7a4d88eae14f10f9fb9dfbcc2d3fea58e34f6077a8eabf1ac7f5d59b63 -->
p245, lines 245-245; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p239; next: p247

> is the mean squared displacement, or MSD, at lag $\ell$. The data estimate it within each microsaccade free segment, then average the available segment estimates at each lag. Larger lags have fewer eligible segments. The 1 ms sampling sets the available lag spacing.[^p2-msd]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p247 sha256 e08773add4a29e0744fabf3e119cbfd086141cccd240e193df9e6cbc59e5504f -->
p247, lines 247-247; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p245; next: p259

> At short lags, the displacement can be smaller than the search coil's measurement error. Suppose its reading is $E_{\rm obs}(t)=E(t)+\nu(t)$, where the measurement error has zero mean, variance $\sigma_m^2$, is independent of true movement, and is uncorrelated between distinct sampled times. For a positive lag,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p259 sha256 e0d20ca4b4a0a1461ac3cdbe79c18120c7eab1f23e8b468e4f451040785f920f -->
p259, lines 259-259; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p247; next: p261

> The mixed movement-error term vanishes by independence. This is Eqs. 18–19. The correction is the variance of a difference of two errors, $2\sigma_m^2$, rather than the variance of a single position error. At exactly zero lag the same error is subtracted from itself, so the MSD is zero. A positive lag plateau near zero lag can still occur when successive measurement errors are independent.[^p2-msd-noise]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p261 sha256 a19a889a1b976783f7fe5467fce2658ff9eb64d3b301975383306afb4c0ee1a8 -->
p261, lines 261-261; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p259; next: p263

> The authors estimated the plateau contribution at order $10^{-3}$ deg$^2$, checked its scale with a coil fixed in space, and subtracted that contribution from the observed MSD. This is a correction of an average statistic, not knowledge of each trial's unknown noise. Smoothing the eye trajectories instead would also change the movement statistics the analysis seeks to measure. If the plateau estimate has uncertainty $10^{-4}$ deg$^2$, a corrected MSD of that size is unreliable even when the plateau itself is much larger. The peer review response uses this example to explain the shortest lag limitation.[^p2-noise-limit]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p263 sha256 3d3ef08daebdef4d2f9c40989a971e5f939a96f451bf400685ca9fc0edd83f3d -->
p263, lines 263-263; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p261; next: p267

> For instance, a constructed measured MSD of $0.0014$ deg$^2$ with an estimated noise contribution $0.0010$ deg$^2$ leaves $0.0004$ deg$^2$. A $0.0001$ deg$^2$ uncertainty in the subtraction is already 25% of that result. This is why a corrected short lag point needs an uncertainty assessment as well as a positive value. If measurement errors were temporally correlated, the correction would instead be $2[\sigma_m^2-C_\nu(\ell)]$, where $C_\nu$ is their covariance at lag $\ell$; a constant subtraction would then need revision.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-FILTERED-MSD -->

### Filtering white noise and filtering Brownian motion differ

Location: line 265, anchor `sec:EYE-FILTERED-MSD`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 267
Closing passage line: 360

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p267 sha256 0101d6c0734265a938f788bc99e58c63c0a11379abe76b55f0ca8fe03f35f881 -->
p267, lines 267-267; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p263; next: p274

> The corrected MSD keeps increasing over the observed range, toward a second, while its logarithmic slope decreases and remains between one and two. Define that local slope by

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p274 sha256 ffa29daf5431a65010c2171d6af6d8b676ba47b86589befb6cf9e5c9f6960a7e -->
p274, lines 274-274; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p267; next: p276

> Ordinary diffusion has $M=2D\ell$ and $\alpha=1$. A roughly constant velocity over a short interval gives displacement $v\ell$ and $M=\mathbb E[v^2]\ell^2$, so $\alpha=2$. Growth faster than linear is called [**superdiffusion**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}. Here it describes a finite range of lags; it does not assert a single power law for arbitrarily long times.[^p2-msd-result]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p276 sha256 e33bf2f79a9c3d2cf4bdf793ee1c0471eceb4ce6775bdd396fe16f43ac37d4e0 -->
p276, lines 276-276; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p274; next: p282

> Could temporally uncorrelated motor noise acquire the observed MSD just by passing through muscles? A [**low pass filter**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} suppresses rapid input changes relative to slow ones. [**White noise**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here is an ideal zero mean input whose fluctuations at different times are uncorrelated. In continuous time its strength is defined by an intensity multiplying a delta function, rather than a finite variance at an individual time. A stable, decaying response to this noise produces a [**stationary process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} in the sense needed here: its mean is constant and its covariance depends only on time separation. Its position variance consequently does not grow with absolute time. Let $E=h*\xi$, where

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p282 sha256 c5ee64d67fb4139bb2976e02abe152ebbb6c401c04baf0b20e4e9df6433c5397 -->
p282, lines 282-282; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p276; next: p289

> The noise intensity $A$ has units input$^2$ times seconds. Expanding the convolution and using the delta function yields, for $\ell\ge0$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p289 sha256 cce01d885c41ed45bcd0e3c82740bd4087c5ba040a3f17e8aa7c755315402583 -->
p289, lines 289-289; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p282; next: p295

> For a stationary centered position, expanding the squared difference then gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p295 sha256 8dc77a94cdb03285ca5537b9157718a263d3f99c8f7a4c0cabc3f62933e00f18 -->
p295, lines 295-295; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p289; next: p297

> If the kernel decays sufficiently rapidly, its overlap with a shifted copy tends to zero. The MSD consequently approaches $2C_E(0)$, a finite plateau. This reconstructs the reasoning of Eqs. 20–22.[^p2-white-noise]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p297 sha256 cfd38f1eba8c38832cd580dd38010703c95906e17fdb2bfce345f9797258724a -->
p297, lines 297-297; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p295; next: p304

> For the one exponential filter $h(u)=\Theta(u)e^{-u/\tau}/\tau$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p304 sha256 567a6f8b08ffe904a9cd0be0f622edfb43ed136a86dbdb6dba94c99e7a72861a -->
p304, lines 304-304; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p297; next: p306

> This simple filter has a short lag MSD proportional to $\ell$ and a long lag plateau. A double exponential filter is smoother at short lags, but still gives a plateau because its kernel also decays. Increasing $A$ raises the curve without removing its flattening. The paper's simulated private OMN noise, passed through muscle dynamics, is both too small and too flat at long lags. In Fig. 3b, using 2,000 heterogeneous OMNs, its contribution is about 1% of the observed variance near 900 ms.[^p2-msd-result]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p306 sha256 0eaa6747317025286196d97819920f989b6ffaf105cedda032a8e16fd2f9531a -->
p306, lines 306-306; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p304; next: p308

> Now suppose the input is a stored position $B(t)$ undergoing Brownian motion with $\mathbb E[(B(t+\ell)-B(t))^2]=2D\ell$. Its position variance accumulates. A low pass filter with nonzero static gain smooths the path while retaining that accumulating long time displacement variance. The following one exponential example is a teaching reconstruction, not the paper's full muscle model. It derives how smooth movement can have a slope between one and two even though the stored position undergoes ordinary diffusion.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p308 sha256 e947a3d4f4a1b23611f8aa204b2bb149785cfb02f5ccc687d1eb10b059e0d5aa -->
p308, lines 308-308; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p306; next: p314

> Let

p314, lines 314-314; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p308; next: p320

> $W$ is a Brownian process with unit increment variance per unit time, so $D$ has units deg$^2$/s. Define velocity $v=\dot E=(B-E)/\tau$. Taking its increment gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p320 sha256 5282ab793765fc39b4e9df56c74dc171e7d37e79ac45138ae4011e83fe52e20c -->
p320, lines 320-320; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p314; next: p328

> Velocity loses its past value over time $\tau$ and receives new random input. After its velocity transient has decayed, its solution is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p328 sha256 77982f2934e213bed74656dc75146d62d0d0dc6fd78039297d985a7132009ce4 -->
p328, lines 328-328; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p320; next: p339

> The covariance follows by multiplying the two integrals: only their shared increments contribute, with the same exponential overlap as in the white noise calculation. Integrating velocity over an interval gives position change. The double integral over its covariance reduces to

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p339 sha256 9e017f94ea3a08ca3f895cb3f314a9531f130a3879b2a838c87dd868655fd426 -->
p339, lines 339-339; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p328; next: p345

> At short lags, $1-e^{-\ell/\tau}=\ell/\tau-\ell^2/(2\tau^2)+\cdots$, so

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p345 sha256 d88a03ac030220b4957eb65cbe06b4f6921ffa0944c5c18e7f1f0b8eff7081b6 -->
p345, lines 345-345; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p339; next: p351

> The eye keeps approximately the same velocity over that interval. At long lags,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p351 sha256 d91f634421f125a5144cad031e4f506e8cbfee428b6993a1a1b8e71edb860a17 -->
p351, lines 351-351; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p345; next: p358

> which grows linearly rather than approaching a plateau. Its local slope is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p358 sha256 f29b95bd4cc0c9a4f048367ca7122106a00151b9360febdc88c252c8ef2d69c8 -->
p358, lines 358-358; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p351; next: p360

> approaching two at short lags and one at long lags. At $\ell=\tau$ it is $e-1\simeq1.718$; at $\ell=5\tau$ it is about $1.239$. Thus an intermediate slope can arise from filtering ordinary diffusion, without faster than Brownian accumulation inside the memory circuit.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p360 sha256 7a1b0ab6a5e4cd83c686d7a37e7dfdba33320bd8e5aa2c18c60e02364b3cfec1 -->
p360, lines 360-360; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p358; next: p364

> The actual muscle response contains fast and slow exponential components, and the full model also contains motor neuron filtering and feedback. Those ingredients change the detailed curve. The one exponential derivation teaches the distinction relevant to Fig. 3: a decaying low pass filter of stationary white noise saturates, whereas the same filter, with nonzero static gain, retains accumulating displacement from a wandering position. A slow correlated peripheral source is a different hypothesis from the temporally uncorrelated noise rejected by this comparison.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL -->

### The proposed circuit from memory to movement

Location: line 362, anchor `sec:EYE-CIRCUIT-MODEL`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 364
Closing passage line: 440

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p364 sha256 eacb0612bb83ad26dc3c1da2b1afeda1529552493d67197223a2d90d0c15fee9 -->
p364, lines 364-364; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p360; next: p366

> The recording analysis places much of the drive upstream of OMNs. To test whether integrator noise could supply it, the authors build a model from a noisy memory circuit through to the eye. The model adapts an earlier goldfish integrator architecture to primate tuning and variability. There are two populations, with excitation within each and inhibition between them. Their firing increases for opposite directions of gaze. This supplies the recurrent interaction needed to maintain a range of stored positions.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p366 sha256 2c55ee2c6b6164e165046b3628b1610bb123b45dafe2dc76be02dc1cdcf2a568 -->
p366, lines 366-366; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p364; next: p373

> For the right population, the model's rate is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p373 sha256 670a618c0d9e32cdfcced57c0ccebb39ddc8097dbe087aa1f4cefc79375840e2 -->
p373, lines 373-373; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p366; next: p375

> $E_{OI}$ is the internally represented position in degrees, $\zeta_i^R$ is a tuning slope in spikes/(s deg), and $\lambda_i^0$ is a rate offset in spikes/s. Taking the maximum with zero prevents negative firing. $F$ is a feedback input in degrees, defined below. The matching left neuron has the opposite slope, $\zeta_i^L=-\zeta_i^R$. In an active neuron, a change of one degree changes its rate by $\zeta_i$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p375 sha256 0fefbab2856e1562ae4a490a06ce94073bb6cf614e00e721593ea1af74d60be4 -->
p375, lines 375-375; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p373; next: p383

> Spikes produce synaptic activity according to

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p383 sha256 af7902f51d056f623d1f9e3bfe92c3cd163f3085baba4c42d920c06b2e517fc8 -->
p383, lines 383-383; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p375; next: p385

> Here $\xi_i^R$ is a spike train generated at rate $\lambda_i^R$, $\tau_s$ is the synaptic decay time, and $\lambda_t=60$ Hz sets the rate scale in the nonlinear synaptic response. Treating spikes as counts, $X_i$ and $S_i$ are dimensionless, and the weights $\eta_i$ are in degrees. Between spikes, $S_i$ decays exponentially; one spike adds a jump of $1/[\tau_s(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)]$. Replacing spikes by their mean rate gives a drive $\lambda_i^R/(\lambda_t+\lambda_i^R)$ that approaches one as the rate increases. This is the model's saturating synaptic effect, rather than the purely linear synapses of the basic 2012 example.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p385 sha256 644dd42008e965323f60d3c11968b02f946c0972597764d6e0a09d713b40cf1a -->
p385, lines 385-385; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p383; next: p393

> The readout $E_{OI}$ feeds back into the rates, so these equations define a recurrent network. Persistence requires a further condition on that feedback. Set $F=0$ and replace spikes by their mean rates. Define the mean saturated drives at a proposed stored value $z$ by

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p393 sha256 4e85e73974ec9b54a93f3b2d6ada8057939635f18aab8c0a679c6ecf72ab1cf3 -->
p393, lines 393-393; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p385; next: p401

> Multiply each synaptic equation by its readout weight, subtract the left contribution, and sum. Since the same $\tau_s$ applies to every unit, the readout obeys

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p401 sha256 e6a2513f8858d08feee8803fd608767f0d15c0c1779cf42a9163cb098060ee08 -->
p401, lines 401-401; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p393; next: p408

> An exact continuum of persistent readout values would require $G(z)=z$ over the represented range. For a small displacement from a fixed value, Taylor expansion gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p408 sha256 3d4fd9a575f1c0417e726edf74b09503c18f6a1002aa184352294f17464a8fd7 -->
p408, lines 408-408; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p401; next: p410

> Thus $G'(z)=1$ is the local neutral condition for the stored coordinate. Stable directions of the remaining synaptic state are also needed for an attractor. The weights were optimized to approximate the fixed-point relation across $-50$ to $50$ degrees. Residual mismatch can produce deterministic drift, so this finite optimized circuit provides approximate persistence rather than an exactly neutral continuum. The tuning slopes and thresholds were sampled to match reported macaque relationships and variation. With spikes, perturbations along the nearly neutral direction additionally accumulate. This reduction is a teaching derivation from the paper's equations; Figure 4 separates the resulting internal diffusion from later muscle smoothing.[^p2-network]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p410 sha256 bec9b956b359fac96d7d94ee37835ffd88b40ba3cfc1da3207f747ad35d26ab5 -->
p410, lines 410-410; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p408; next: p418

> The motor neurons receive this position signal:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p418 sha256 dc789f13c057db91a7975ce8b20dae47a49a8bfc0a45404054f3ebe109469778 -->
p418, lines 418-418; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p410; next: p420

> $\lambda_i^M$ is a motor rate in spikes/s, $E_{T_i}$ is that neuron's position threshold in degrees, and $k_i$ is its position sensitivity. $\xi_i^M$ is its generated spike train. The filtered output $s_i^M$ also has units spikes/s, with decay time $\tau_{MN}$. One spike makes a jump of $1/\tau_{MN}$; at constant mean rate, the mean output is that rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p420 sha256 ddcb220d0dfb7202998c334244af8f386a828df6a8544acf98825fc373b6d8b8 -->
p420, lines 420-420; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p418; next: p427

> Each motor unit's muscle and plant response is represented by a position contribution $E_i$ satisfying

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p427 sha256 3c2d953faf07527bf3bcdf292f35e5db291ea8ddb0a0835a7b880a1c398e068f -->
p427, lines 427-427; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p420; next: p429

> These are Eqs. 28–31. The second order equation uses the same roles and units as the forward fit. At constant $E_{OI}$, an active unit has mean $s_i^M=k_i(E_{OI}-E_{T_i})$, giving mean $E_i=E_{OI}$ when derivatives vanish. Averaging therefore transmits the common position while reducing independent private fluctuations. This is an effective model of combined motor output, not a claim that one motor unit moves an isolated eye of its own.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p429 sha256 136a8be33cc7785e4a5baecf0bb1832cea6b8f9f8a858e32423042a32d9d832c -->
p429, lines 429-429; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p427; next: p431

> The model used diverse thresholds and response coefficients, with motor thresholds sampled between $-45$ and $-5$ degrees, and imposed $m_i<r_i^2/(4k_i)$ to give nonoscillatory individual response kernels. Main Methods report $N=30{,}000$ integrator neurons, $N_m=1{,}000$ OMNs, and $\tau_s=20$ ms for the main full simulation. The separate private OMN comparison in Fig. 3 used 2,000 OMNs. These neuron counts belong to different simulation comparisons.[^p2-motor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p431 sha256 8f07618e944efcd34835f24a99793429fdb45a9eb8f0aa016cb6bd2a12d40534 -->
p431, lines 431-431; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p429; next: p438

> The final loop is delayed visual feedback:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p438 sha256 157ca33f74a870b3a44e71a52b201d3d89c57dbdd45a3120e4fe723a47144f6f -->
p438, lines 438-438; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p431; next: p440

> Both positions are in degrees, so $A$ is dimensionless in this formulation. The main value is $A=0.015$. If gaze and its internal representation have recently moved rightward, the delayed position is to their left, making $F$ negative. Its contribution to the integrator rates then opposes continued rightward movement. The difference can serve as an estimate of recent movement; it is not an error relative to a fixed absolute target position. For a slowly varying position that the eye tracks, $E(t-\tau_d)-E_{OI}(t)\simeq-\tau_d\dot E(t)$, showing the velocity-like action and sign. The approximation requires tracking and slow change over the delay.[^p2-feedback]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p440 sha256 c748cf8e7e32992c2f33cde2c252ae4998dccb11b1ef9cadf3a0c76263e78fcd -->
p440, lines 440-440; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p438; next: p444

> The effect on the ongoing MSD must be checked from the coupled model, rather than inferred from the delay alone. A short MSD lag is a separation between two samples in a running process, not the time since a new perturbation. Past measurements can already affect both samples, and $F$ also contains the immediate term $-A E_{OI}(t)$. Extended Data Fig. 3 compares the model with and without feedback: feedback changes amplitude across lags, while its larger effect on logarithmic slope occurs at longer lags. Figure 5 shows the full model. Muscle filtering supplies the short-lag smoothness, and feedback improves the detailed longer-lag fit. These are results of this model comparison, not a hard delay-based cutoff or a claim excluding visual control under other tasks.[^p2-feedback]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/04-eye-drift.md::sec:EYE-SCALING -->

### Which neural parameters set the amount of drift?

Location: line 442, anchor `sec:EYE-SCALING`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 444
Closing passage line: 499

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p444 sha256 f09f4822b4f00383ec7978267f819d941ad75bb6448b1670d9ece8a0515ba269 -->
p444, lines 444-444; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p440; next: p446

> An agreement in amplitude is informative only if we know how much freedom the model has. The paper uses regular spike trains. Chapter 1 defined the interspike interval coefficient of variation (CV) as the interval standard deviation divided by its mean. A Poisson process at constant rate has CV one; the model uses about 0.22 for integrator neurons and about 0.07 for OMNs. Such firing is called [**sub-Poisson spiking**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} here because its long window count variability is lower than a Poisson process at the same rate, under the interval construction used in the model.[^p2-cv]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p446 sha256 426484c85a1fa9facac2aadc3757571dcf77e291c22e7490984488a5dad39d4f -->
p446, lines 446-446; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p444; next: p448

> The construction starts with a Poisson process at $M$ times the desired rate and retains every $M$th spike. At constant rate, a retained interval is the sum of $M$ independent exponential intervals. Each initial interval has mean $1/(M\lambda)$ and variance $1/(M\lambda)^2$. The retained interval therefore has mean $1/\lambda$, variance $1/(M\lambda^2)$, and CV $1/\sqrt M$. This preserves the mean rate while reducing variability. Its use at changing rates is part of the model; the simple interval derivation assumes a constant rate.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p448 sha256 1c34aa437a2473f6ff12b4d03a98c480e59c43356f8cf27507f3da19fe1cf438 -->
p448, lines 448-448; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p446; next: p456

> At constant rate, the retained intervals are independent. This is a [**renewal process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, a spike process whose successive intervals are independent and identically distributed. Let their mean be $\mu=1/\lambda$ and standard deviation $\sigma_I$. The time to $n$ spikes has variance $n\sigma_I^2$. For a long fixed window $L\simeq n\mu$, a fluctuation in total interval time produces the opposite fluctuation in count divided by $\mu$. Thus

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p456 sha256 fd539d6cd95199881e440ea6cd79ae904219dced0a907f2ef92959a7a2454a2c -->
p456, lines 456-456; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p448; next: p458

> This explains why the diffusion scale contains CV squared: it is the strength of the accumulated spike fluctuations relative to the Poisson case. Chapters 2 and 3 show how projecting those fluctuations onto a neutral memory direction adds a factor proportional to $1/\tau_s^2$ and, for a comparable independent population, $1/N$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p458 sha256 ea92a5944f6936bf14c08b8eb5a686309ccd3a64074dcb0fbd8b0fd3d02a5a2f -->
p458, lines 458-458; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p456; next: p464

> Rate and sensitivity need more care. Write the absolute local rate slope as $a=d\lambda/dE$, in spikes/(s deg). If $N$ similar cells fire near rate $\lambda_0$, their Poisson information per unit time about position is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p464 sha256 a3c872aa2d78f6bffb65fc539964c2c369a494bce152d719874c979b550e6033 -->
p464, lines 464-464; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p458; next: p471

> Using the information-diffusion relation of Chapter 3 as a scale, including the $\mathrm{CV}^2$ factor just derived and holding the network geometry fixed,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p471 sha256 a97e949c6ae8180c1ec415fd1e133720415886dedd04e3fdde70ef41afb02e93 -->
p471, lines 471-471; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p464; next: p477

> If instead sensitivity is expressed as the fractional slope $b=a/\lambda_0$, in inverse degrees, the same expression becomes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p477 sha256 1cfeeb6408d3bedd5ab3a9a7edbcbac20b11c45ae6b9d6ebc4cc9f77f751e71e -->
p477, lines 477-479; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p471; next: p481

> This second form has the dependence printed in the paper's Eq. 1, $D\propto\mathrm{CV}^2N^{-1}\lambda_0^{-1}k_0^{-2}\tau_s^{-2}$. However, its surrounding text calls $k_0$ position sensitivity without specifying fractional normalization. If $k_0$ means an absolute slope in Hz/degree, that printed expression has the wrong units for $D$; the version with $\lambda_0$ in the numerator has the required units deg$^2$/s. The guide therefore distinguishes $a$ and $b$ rather than silently treating the printed $k_0$ as unambiguous. This scaling is a comparison under fixed network geometry and noise assumptions, not an exact formula for every recurrent circuit.[^p2-scaling]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p481 sha256 241a99addca6b8e7b023147c43e48e7a3523e9b0d52a50a7a021c64f11be65c9 -->
p481, lines 481-481; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p477; next: p483

> Doubling $N$ halves this diffusion scale; doubling CV multiplies it by four; doubling $\tau_s$ divides it by four. Doubling the absolute slope at fixed rate also divides it by four. Increasing rate reduces diffusion when the fractional slope is fixed, because more spikes then also supply a proportionally steeper absolute slope. Increasing rate at fixed absolute slope is a different comparison and increases the scale in the first formula. A rate change alone does not specify which comparison is intended.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p483 sha256 96cd80b8d962767580705fe9c3c707439e072e0abeb2940437a471e7a6524b04 -->
p483, lines 483-483; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p481; next: p490

> Could the measured CV itself mostly reflect drift rather than private spike variability? Supplementary Notes test this. Let $\lambda(t)=\bar\lambda+aB(t)$ during a fixation of length $T$, with the rate changes small relative to $\bar\lambda$. For a Brownian path starting at zero, the expected time averaged squared position is $DT$, and the expected squared time mean is $2DT/3$. Subtracting gives the expected within-segment position variance $DT/3=2DT/6$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p490 sha256 72378fdd398d6e247d1a7cc8ca3b5e733fe43ff9b996e8ca59dadc25f329176a -->
p490, lines 490-492; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p483; next: p499

> The second term follows from $\mathbb E[B(t)B(s)]=2D\min(t,s)$ and $T^{-2}\int_0^T\int_0^T2D\min(t,s)\,dt\,ds=2DT/3$. Expanding $1/(\bar\lambda+aB)$ to first order gives $1/\bar\lambda-aB/\bar\lambda^2$. Dividing its standard deviation by its mean then gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p499 sha256 e5b6617e73dc132c58fd249a77b9dba9c1d4461bbaac83be5095e135cc171db0 -->
p499, lines 499-499; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p490; next: p503

> With the supplement's values $a=1/15$ spikes/(s arcmin), $2D=100$ arcmin$^2$/s, $T=0.5$ s, and $\bar\lambda=80$ Hz, this is about $0.0024$, well below the OMN CV near 0.07. These are an order of magnitude check, not a fitted diffusion constant for the whole model. Since one degree is 60 arcmin, that $2D$ equals $0.0278$ deg$^2$/s, or $D=0.0139$ deg$^2$/s.[^p2-cv-drift]

> The most useful supplemental comparisons are Extended Data Fig. 1 for individual uncertainty, Fig. 2 for noise subtraction and its simulated control, Fig. 3 for feedback, Fig. 4 for a no-central-drive control, and Fig. 5 for the other monkey's fit. In the no-central-drive control, $\widehat\chi=-0.09\pm0.22$ is compatible with zero; that negative estimate is sampling variation. Figures 6–8 document cell classification, segment sampling, and a rank correlation check. Figure 9 shows the heterogeneous model parameters. Figure 10 separates spiking noise passed directly to the output from spiking noise that drives diffusion inside the integrator: internal stochastic spikes remain in the latter comparison. Its caption reports little direct output-noise effect beyond 50 ms.[^p2-supp-figures]

### What remains uncertain

Location: line 515, anchor `sec:EYE-LIMITS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 517

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p517 sha256 876534e2f608dba2b7b412a30b96f21cd84ab3537d225965e6312832209ee211 -->
p517, lines 517-517; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p513; next: p519

> The data and model answer different localization questions. The common variance estimate supports a drive upstream of OMNs, given the calibration and noise assumptions. The MSD comparison supports a command that accumulates variation over the measured interval, rather than stationary white noise passed through a decaying muscle filter. The circuit model shows that integrator noise with plausible parameters can generate such a command and explain its filtered amplitude and timing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p519 sha256 65e828069ea0991be0e835e6830502455675274f5dfeb53a94b894aa57258d29 -->
p519, lines 519-519; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p517; next: p521

> Those results do not uniquely locate the noise inside integrator neurons. No integrator neurons were recorded in this study. No selective change of that circuit tested whether it was necessary for drift. Noisy inputs to the integrator, or an already diffusive upstream signal, remain possible. The authors make this distinction in the Discussion and explicitly in their peer review response. Several combinations of neuron count, variability, and synaptic time constant can also fit an amplitude, so matching one curve does not uniquely recover those parameters.[^p2-localization]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p521 sha256 e90133b47eae4fd2b41bea14ed4fbcd84fab6245e8e39ca2a6b45d74f07524b8 -->
p521, lines 521-521; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p519; next: none

> The study's empirical scope is horizontal motion during these fixation segments in two animals. Visual tasks, feedback, and other sources of input can alter the statistics. Its contribution to the connection with the 2012 theory is more precise: a continuous memory can diffuse internally, while a mechanical output of that memory looks smooth and superdiffusive at shorter lags. To compare a memory theory with behavior, the mapping from stored value to measured movement is part of the calculation.

## ch:PLACE-FIELDS - Why place fields have different sizes and shapes

Source file: `content/chapters/05-place-fields.md`

Purpose: A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?

Prerequisites: ch:FOUNDATIONS

Sidecar sections: From a rate map to a random function, Count fields by counting crossings, Infer a threshold and a spatial scale, Why rare high fields are nearly parabolic, Derive the size law in each dimension, Why slices are a separate test, Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh, Count holes as well as connected fields, What was fitted and what was tested, What Gaussian maps imply about synaptic input

Blocks: 0 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

p3, lines 3-3; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: none; next: p5

> A cell recorded on a short track may have one smooth firing hill. On a long track, the same kind of cell may have several separated fields, each with a different width and height. In a room, its active regions can bend, split, and surround inactive holes. How could one model produce this variety and still make useful predictions?

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p5 sha256 fd9fd365a43ffa239f2316b9104d766f576a9e7739754f0fd417dd1ba3c16e72 -->
p5, lines 5-5; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p7

> Mainali, Azeredo da Silveira, and Burak propose that a cell's spatial input is a smooth random function. The cell fires where this function exceeds a threshold. The model predicts distributions of field lengths, areas, and volumes, rather than the location of a particular cell's next field. Its strongest tests ask whether fitting field sizes and counts also explains field shapes and holes.[^p3-question]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 5af35d8c1714d35ee4fc266e2136c92dc67bbc7efb5b5c47f75bf70c63088be1 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p5; next: p11

> This chapter develops the calculations behind that claim. Spatial randomness here describes different possible rate maps. Once a map is drawn, it is fixed while the animal moves through it. The spatial coordinate is not time, and the model does not by itself describe memory drift, spike timing, or learning.

### From a rate map to a random function

Location: line 9, anchor `sec:PLACE-GP`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 11
Closing passage line: 56

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p11 sha256 cf3931630bd66d71de8518d443fc4b71f41ade0a3e68c41d522d4c3108417994 -->
p11, lines 11-11; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p13

> To predict a field's width, we need to relate activity at nearby positions. A histogram of firing rates alone loses that information: rearranging its values could turn one broad field into many tiny fields without changing the histogram.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p13 sha256 6bf4ed6cec6671e28df85e0d8c8761fece961e5d77b2583573a2795f97375957 -->
p13, lines 13-13; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p11; next: p20

> A [**Gaussian process**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is a probability distribution over functions such that the values at any finite set of positions form a jointly Gaussian random vector. For positions $x_1,\ldots,x_n$, the vector $(h(x_1),\ldots,h(x_n))$ therefore has a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Its mean vector and covariance matrix are obtained by evaluating two functions,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p20 sha256 419066832bea6d8516c5b20b300f3dc252834384d9e2d509f0f67b011cc70a60 -->
p20, lines 20-20; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p13; next: p22

> The expectation runs over possible maps, or over cells assumed to share these statistics. These two functions determine every finite joint distribution. A Gaussian process can thus produce many different curves while keeping a precise rule for their spatial dependence. A jointly Gaussian vector is one for which every linear combination of its entries is Gaussian. For this family, zero covariance between two subvectors implies their independence. That extra property, which does not hold for general distributions, will simplify the crossing calculation. The values after thresholding will generally not have a Gaussian distribution.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p22 sha256 0065786efb64a4ae3a2e015a31a61c1c8807381e1cd6b52e55dd63bca7b6653e -->
p22, lines 22-22; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p20; next: p24

> Three assumptions have different jobs. [**Stationarity**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that moving the observation window does not change the map's probability law. Here it gives a constant mean and a covariance $C(x,y)=C(y-x)$ that depends on separation. It does not say that a particular map is flat. [**Isotropy**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} means that rotating the separation does not change the covariance: in several dimensions, $C(\Delta x)$ depends only on $\|\Delta x\|$. It rules out a preferred direction in the model's statistics. Stationarity alone does not imply isotropy. Finally, smoothness controls whether the map has well defined spatial derivatives. Crossing calculations below require differentiable sample functions and nondegenerate joint distributions: the relevant vector of heights and derivatives must have an invertible covariance matrix, rather than obey an exact linear constraint. Peak calculations require second derivatives and enough regularity to control their local approximation. A Gaussian distribution of values does not guarantee this smoothness. For example, a covariance proportional to $e^{-|x-y|/b}$ has a sharp corner at zero separation and does not supply the differentiable paths assumed below.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p24 sha256 f19aba453f15007b3477fd225c1ceac7a62a4b58c1fa0af170d4819308371576 -->
p24, lines 24-24; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p22; next: p32

> Set the mean to zero and write $\sigma_h^2=C(0)$ for input variance. A constant input mean can be absorbed into the threshold. It is useful to normalize the input,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p32 sha256 ce7eb0a4f37f7c549990ceb25320beb5eaa6985bcc1dda9d3463fb491977207b -->
p32, lines 32-32; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p24; next: p34

> Here $z$ has variance one, $q$ is a dimensionless threshold, and $a>0$ converts normalized input to firing rate, in Hz. The paper writes $\theta$ for $q$, $r$ for covariance, and $D$ for spatial dimension; this guide uses $q,C,d$ to avoid conflicts with earlier chapters. The original input threshold is $q\sigma_h$. In the paper's Equation 3 the firing rate is proportional to $[h-q\sigma_h]_+$, which is equivalent to this form after choosing the gain.[^p3-model]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p34 sha256 9592ff2aea10761d39a66b053c3f004cb74088f339cf18ac94f3d60936b149ce -->
p34, lines 34-34; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p32; next: p40

> The [**excursion set**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} above $q$ is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p40 sha256 67c6eb073c01f241c6cd6231337a5a3a819177f30602af0c509d4c3fec7df59c -->
p40, lines 40-40; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p34; next: p42

> Each connected piece of $A_q$ is a model place field. In one dimension its size is length. In two and three dimensions it is area and volume. Two nearby peaks belong to one field if an above-threshold path joins them. Counting peaks and counting fields therefore answer different questions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p42 sha256 13a7443113bd7235868647853097382186a2f42f844defb231ad1430a9d7d59a -->
p42, lines 42-42; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p40; next: p49

> For a one dimensional stationary process, differentiation of covariance gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p49 sha256 2442f626a333929c7774a980d8033033c9da5bffa9887ab195db0428a595466a -->
p49, lines 49-49; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p42; next: p56

> The minus sign comes from differentiating $y-x$ once with respect to each variable. The paper defines the spatial scale

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p56 sha256 766c616e0154b3bb719c9924855db5e4e8fa62cfd08baac4f9c34f39bbdc3798 -->
p56, lines 56-56; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p49; next: p60

> Thus $\sigma$ compares typical height with typical slope and has units of distance. In an isotropic field, the same definition applies along any unit direction. For the smooth Gaussian covariance $C(\Delta x)=\sigma_h^2\exp[-\|\Delta x\|^2/(2\sigma^2)]$, it is exactly the parameter in that exponent. The paper uses a Gaussian covariance for its simulations. Other smooth covariances can share this local scale while having different longer distance structure.[^p3-scale]

### Count fields by counting crossings

Location: line 58, anchor `sec:PLACE-KAC-RICE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 60
Closing passage line: 106

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p60 sha256 856ec0de26ed8e343059f6a30d4a33b37addb1f75924df0542bd202348000784 -->
p60, lines 60-60; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p56; next: p62

> Finding every root of a random curve is difficult. Counting how often the curve passes a level is easier because a small interval around a crossing has a width determined by its slope.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p62 sha256 89750673492344d837763a92c89b3cd3f64839f3a984d2cf64b05477dd7eb0ef -->
p62, lines 62-62; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p60; next: p68

> Consider a differentiable fixed curve and a simple upward crossing $x_0$ of $q$, with slope $v=z'(x_0)>0$. Close to the crossing,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p68 sha256 694e8b08f760f19502fd22105dd04cf5c551d8854bcb6e89d52759c7627596f1 -->
p68, lines 68-68; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p62; next: p74

> The curve lies in the vertical band $|z-q|<\varepsilon$ for a spatial distance approximately $2\varepsilon/v$. Integrating $z'(x)/(2\varepsilon)$ over that interval contributes one. Restricting to positive slopes counts only upward crossings. Under the usual regularity conditions, averaging this counting identity gives the [**Kac–Rice formula**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} for the mean upcrossing density,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p74 sha256 cebf0d41e064a8bad6910b7100d9094edae28aeb69aa714144b9f245bce64d7e -->
p74, lines 74-74; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p68; next: p76

> The density $p_{z,z'}(q,v)$ describes how often height is near $q$ and slope near $v$. Multiplication by $v$ compensates for the shorter spatial window of a steep crossing. Merely multiplying the probability of being above threshold by a typical slope would miss this weighting.[^p3-crossings]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p76 sha256 8c29e2b994c75073980c3f8ad32714b819c26ccc7b35bd53d2de20a9b6433dc4 -->
p76, lines 76-76; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p74; next: p82

> We can evaluate the integral because height and slope are independent here. Stationarity gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p82 sha256 84fa962b3b57a4ad77ef7daa267fe687fa4c1e5f87b1c643090fb3b1ab3d7c5a -->
p82, lines 82-82; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p76; next: p96

> Height and slope are jointly Gaussian; zero covariance therefore makes them independent. Their distributions are $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ and $z'\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^{-2})$. Consequently,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p96 sha256 55ce3b5cdfc08d42f8cd193bc859364fc8b1da4765231b569c8820e7d09e5377 -->
p96, lines 96-96; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p82; next: p98

> In the integral, use $u=\sigma^2v^2/2$, so $v\,dv=du/\sigma^2$. This result is exact for the stated smooth stationary Gaussian model. A high threshold is not needed. Its units are inverse distance: increasing $\sigma$ stretches the map and reduces crossings per metre.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p98 sha256 a3178d05daf5a8383331b6a0a5b1c5e79a3b9e27e140138effb0735de2e92ce6 -->
p98, lines 98-98; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p96; next: p106

> On a finite track $[0,L]$, each upcrossing starts a field, but a field may already be active at the left endpoint. If $N_L$ counts all components intersecting the track, then, under regularity conditions that exclude tangencies and give nonzero slopes at crossings,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p106 sha256 7736661f9fb1708487ba89d75f45e334b953e0f0ba02c2c2a3f68d5fa0f24dad -->
p106, lines 106-106; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p98; next: p110

> where $Q(q)=1-\Phi(q)$ and $\Phi$ is the standard normal cumulative distribution function. The last term is the endpoint correction. Removing incomplete boundary fields changes the counting convention, so the same correction cannot then be used unchanged. This identity also gives the paper's exact one dimensional mean Euler characteristic, Equation 8.[^p3-ec-source]

### Infer a threshold and a spatial scale

Location: line 108, anchor `sec:PLACE-INFERENCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 110
Closing passage line: 153

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p110 sha256 5d7d5dc39403425b5fd9abe772ce85e311e8b051d71dda011ea792a94bb785ee -->
p110, lines 110-110; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p106; next: p112

> A low field count can result from a high threshold or a slowly varying map. Field lengths help separate those possibilities.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p112 sha256 3ba9275df1f7ac4137a1a3e303c3f773156791a58bfb30831959120f36dbfd87 -->
p112, lines 112-112; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p110; next: p123

> At a uniformly chosen position, the probability of activity is $Q(q)$. Hence a track of length $L$ has expected occupied length $LQ(q)$. On a long stationary track, the average occupied length per field is the occupied length per unit distance divided by the number of fields per unit distance. The corresponding gap calculation uses the inactive fraction $\Phi(q)$:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p123 sha256 6fc750fb0ae48d8804aa2e0a57ba3d7dfaf42a38e0f59cb9564ec1a8f7ab25fd -->
p123, lines 123-123; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p112; next: p125

> These are stationary per-field and per-gap means, ignoring clipping at track boundaries. They are not an assertion that the expectation of a finite sample's ratio equals the ratio of its expectations. Subject to suitable averaging over a long track or many maps, they give the inference rule in Methods S3, Equations S14–S15.[^p3-means]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p125 sha256 302cfe3ed35e922f0fd2c191f88efb35fc98f0654a85332d71b8cd317ac46e33 -->
p125, lines 125-125; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p123; next: p132

> Adding and dividing removes one unknown at a time:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p132 sha256 735730f52e5c6f810adc36cacc71e4c2fa20715b18de9638770a73cd88db0e2e -->
p132, lines 132-132; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p125; next: p140

> Thus

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p140 sha256 a4f2ea777c7db9fea0029a5b14dc58a73452ea71201e76fc031fbcc2c66d9ab7 -->
p140, lines 140-140; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p132; next: p142

> For example, $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m, the fitted parameters reported for the 200 m bat tunnel, give $Q(q)=0.1357$, $\nu_+=0.03621$ per metre, $\mu_s=3.746$ m, and $\mu_g=23.868$ m in the continuous model. Putting these two means into the inversion recovers $q=1.1$ and $\sigma=2.4$ m. The expected number of intersecting fields on a 200 m track is $7.2425+0.1357=7.3782$, including the left endpoint term. These are calculations from the fitted model, not fresh estimates from raw recordings.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p142 sha256 5603218cf6641f79a36df6dd40cf50b7ff3a2209c4a3cdd48bcf092341d01a6c -->
p142, lines 142-142; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p140; next: p144

> The means do not imply a full gap or count distribution. At high thresholds, distant rare excursions can approach independent events if correlations decay sufficiently with distance. In that regime, field locations approach a Poisson process, counts in a long interval are approximately Poisson, and gaps are approximately exponential. Smoothness and stationarity alone do not ensure this independence. A periodic Gaussian process, for example, retains long distance dependence. Upward and downward crossings that enclose the same small field remain strongly related even when fields far apart are almost independent.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p144 sha256 d8329140074a8cfa78d595d1fb497bf52c7bb51a0b4851af51cb62d749a92fe0 -->
p144, lines 144-144; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p142; next: p153

> Experimental selection also matters. If cells with no fields are excluded, a Poisson model with unconditioned mean $\lambda$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p153 sha256 3889a03387b2f0ea6253d52ac9eddfcfc693e052e1672ed237823cf0423ca7d5 -->
p153, lines 153-153; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p144; next: p157

> The mean follows because the omitted $n=0$ term contributes zero to $\sum n\Pr(N=n)=\lambda$, while conditioning divides all remaining probabilities by $1-e^{-\lambda}$. For $\lambda=1$, $36.8\%$ of cells are excluded and the observed mean is $1.582$, not one. Using $1.582$ as the unconditioned mean would infer too many fields. The paper applies this correction to its two dimensional count fit, Equation 5.[^p3-selection]
