### Why rare high fields are nearly parabolic

Location: line 155, anchor `sec:PLACE-RARE-PEAKS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 157
Closing passage line: 214

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p157 sha256 8a213a9a4cdb7b9dc8ab0be133e695b548461eae57144ad250b758db6d0c99b3 -->
p157, lines 157-157; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p153; next: p159

> The crossing calculation determines a mean length exactly, but it does not yet explain the shape of the length histogram. To obtain that distribution, we examine the small region around a peak that barely exceeds a high threshold.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p159 sha256 7a06a8966fc407864dc239fdf7f8047062e2c0db36bfde22a6096aaeee9baa24 -->
p159, lines 159-159; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p157; next: p161

> The following is a teaching reconstruction of the high excursion argument in Methods S4, with algebraic corrections made explicit below. It assumes a sufficiently smooth, stationary, isotropic, unit variance Gaussian field, an invertible covariance matrix for the relevant derivative variables, and $q\to\infty$. It describes typical components counted once each, away from environment boundaries. It is not a proof of the full excursion convergence theorem for every allowed covariance.[^p3-rare-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p161 sha256 5008ba9c5518f8b90d29824bba9fcdbb0eb1bda6e2da6e0a5b449b5c20543698 -->
p161, lines 161-161; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p159; next: p168

> The [**Hessian**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} is the matrix $H$ of second spatial derivatives; its eigenvalues describe curvature along the principal directions. Isotropy and the definition of $\sigma$ imply

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p168 sha256 c8fb27ef5a829c9fdf7d0b0566b33f765c33eaa10ee7f51ed1bd1071f0d292f0 -->
p168, lines 168-168; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p161; next: p174

> Here $\delta_{ij}$ is one for equal indices and zero otherwise. For jointly Gaussian variables, conditioning $Y$ on a scalar $Z$ of variance one gives $\mathbb E[Y\mid Z=b]=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)b$ when both means vanish. One way to see this is to write $Y=\operatorname{Cov}(Y,Z)Z+\epsilon$; the remainder has zero covariance with $Z$ and is therefore independent of it. Applying this to each Hessian entry gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p174 sha256 47d4b1cf84b128232a6a68f53843a3ec5ff5371866328fc9cd3b8a9d22498674 -->
p174, lines 174-174; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p168; next: p176

> The conditional fluctuations around this mean have a variance that does not grow with $b$. For a high peak, the negative mean curvature is of order $q$, so its relative fluctuations become small. Conditioning the gradient to zero does not change this Gaussian conditional mean, because the gradient has zero covariance with both height and Hessian in a stationary isotropic field. Selecting an actual maximum also weights curvatures, but its leading high peak shape is the same.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p176 sha256 0041a3d93b0436aee52ecdbbc68f4786f15bd183b547921f61c99d063d378053 -->
p176, lines 176-176; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p174; next: p182

> Let the peak height be $q+\epsilon$, with $\epsilon>0$, and put the peak at the origin. The gradient there is zero. The Taylor expansion becomes, to leading order,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p182 sha256 ce43d13cbc80cb3597d592e48da77bda24354b1356839541482fd1700805ad48 -->
p182, lines 182-182; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p176; next: p188

> It is positive inside an approximately round region of radius

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p188 sha256 149703e61581dbce167ad949599b9142ab8467195fdf771e4fcd9415784f6603 -->
p188, lines 188-188; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p182; next: p195

> The remaining question is how $\epsilon$ is distributed when we select one high field at a time. In one dimension, the number of high maxima is asymptotically the number of upcrossings, proportional to $e^{-q^2/2}$. Therefore the fraction of peaks above $q$ that also exceed $q+\epsilon$ is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p195 sha256 9d42766e0fc8603f0a98d7761de1a9ef6a3caafc35dc5d516e261062df577e81 -->
p195, lines 195-195; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p188; next: p202

> Define $U=q\epsilon$. At fixed $u\ge0$ and as $q$ increases,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p202 sha256 f920ad413a21fd0678e4a37f81ae70b08f491b1bfaa09eb0641891a0bd84de13 -->
p202, lines 202-202; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p195; next: p204

> Thus the scaled excess height approaches an exponential variable of mean one. In several dimensions, high peak counts have the additional leading factor $q^{d-1}$. Its ratio at $q+u/q$ and $q$ tends to one, so the same excess-height limit remains. The higher dimensional count factor will also follow from the Euler characteristic calculation below. A rigorous conclusion requires control of the remainder and of components containing multiple peaks, beyond this local argument.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p204 sha256 2deb6f24b242404c3ab3dfa2f2642f337252e20239c3140667a1b04d3b4953b6 -->
p204, lines 204-204; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p202; next: p212

> In one dimension, the full field length is $s=2r_*$. Eliminating $\epsilon$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p212 sha256 f09592e651a2e22940edc8fdd80e9c441e4f24aa4697d81d93b30a1b077d999e -->
p212, lines 212-212; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p204; next: p214

> This supplies two distinct predictions. The length is a scaled square root of an exponential variable, giving a Rayleigh distribution. The peak firing rate is $a\epsilon$, so at fixed high $q$ it is proportional to $s^2$. The exponent two concerns rate versus linear size. In three dimensions a suitable linear size is $s^{1/3}$ when $s$ denotes volume.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p214 sha256 acdee6f644c343b31dd07894bc92b9af954e704fe99f475ff3b9568012cc8f2e -->
p214, lines 214-214; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p212; next: p218

> At moderate thresholds the omitted shape fluctuations matter. Figure 3C reports a rate versus length exponent of $1.42$ in the 200 m bat data and $1.46$ in finite threshold model simulations. Figure 3D reports $1.75$ versus $1.71$ using cube root of volume in 3D. These are compatible with a model that approaches exponent two only as the threshold becomes large; they are not measurements of the limiting exponent. Figure 5A–C illustrates the progression toward simpler, usually single peak fields as $q$ increases. Figure 4C tests this trend in the shorter bat tunnel.[^p3-shapes]


Claim-bearing content: none

Claims to check:

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW -->

### Derive the size law in each dimension

Location: line 216, anchor `sec:PLACE-SIZE-LAW`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 218
Closing passage line: 271

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p218 sha256 1901fc4e63d3a5b50382c5e7752d17da5da6af0c255516e359814c81b92aeae6 -->
p218, lines 218-218; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p214; next: p220

> A round region's radius and its volume have different units and different distributions. This geometric conversion explains why the model predicts different histograms in a track, an arena, and a room.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p220 sha256 58f2100b678e20db0a4cacb98880ede96dd10f963881a764df0d3e7cfb46986f -->
p220, lines 220-220; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p218; next: p222

> Let $v_d=\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(1+d/2)$ be the volume of a unit ball in $d$ dimensions. For $d=1,2,3$, it is $2,\pi,4\pi/3$. The symbol $\Gamma$ is the gamma function, defined for $t>0$ by $\Gamma(t)=\int_0^\infty u^{t-1}e^{-u}\,du$. It extends factorials: $\Gamma(n+1)=n!$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p222 sha256 2622397a97e4137834c9aecff9dee82065045328ad11e47745542a699a321117 -->
p222, lines 222-222; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p220; next: p231

> Using the radius from the preceding section, the field size is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p231 sha256 d7015fd40a4d70397a639649766ed3dab80232ac55cec5c395bb28e2c7e89e0c -->
p231, lines 231-231; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p222; next: p240

> Since the density of $U$ is $e^{-u}$ for $u>0$, changing variables gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p240 sha256 a21700950f13cea61955cf68feb5958b2567b3e5be5df2609be8d48ac0ec2752 -->
p240, lines 240-240; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p231; next: p242

> This is the paper's Equation 1. The expression for $\beta$ above is its leading high threshold geometric scale, not an exact finite threshold identity. A finite threshold simulation can share the approximate shape while having a different fitted scale.[^p3-size-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p242 sha256 bd78903b899f2ba416d0a9fa0e50e6848ecdffcdf5af8bfb1f67d84cbd4075bb -->
p242, lines 242-242; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p240; next: p250

> Normalization needs no special integral: $\int_0^\infty p_d(s)\,ds=\int_0^\infty e^{-u}\,du=1$. The same substitution supplies every positive moment,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p250 sha256 68d166b7bef6c9ba2e0f4f11914f00a8227a3ee28343df99cb6bc8fe6d84747e -->
p250, lines 250-250; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p242; next: p257

> In particular,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p257 sha256 e0cefd2bef21d8c57c06ba03819d1f74f6d0f41e3ad03b4ed921afc4a741b404 -->
p257, lines 257-257; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p250; next: p265

> Size has units of distance to the power $d$, while $\beta$ has units of inverse distance squared in every dimension. This makes $\beta s^{2/d}$ dimensionless, and makes $p_d(s)$ a density per unit size. These checks catch mistakes in changing variables.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p265 sha256 b0cac387c1042983a3962b519a03aca42a4d5f9b880a93c1b0417150203c90f8 -->
p265, lines 265-265; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p257; next: p267

> A [**Weibull distribution**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"} has survival probability $\exp[-(s/b)^k]$ for size $s>0$, scale $b>0$, and shape $k>0$. Here $k=2/d$ and $b=\beta^{-d/2}$. In 3D the diverging density does not mean a positive probability of exactly zero volume; the probability of $0<s<\delta$ still tends to zero as $\delta\to0$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p267 sha256 1198b5a52fcf5236f4556770b0651c726dee0edcbce5726be8f473d67ea222db -->
p267, lines 267-267; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p265; next: p269

> For a concrete comparison, set $\sigma=1$ m and $q=5$ in this limiting calculation. The mean size is $0.5013$ m in 1D, $0.2513$ m$^2$ in 2D, and $0.1260$ m$^3$ in 3D. These values describe the same local distance scale and threshold, rather than fields with the same numerical size in different units. More generally, the geometry and moment formula simplify to $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p269 sha256 1b6a73736b83824e1f2dce869a030143c3a059b73ec4bade01713e236c0f32fc -->
p269, lines 269-269; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p267; next: p271

> This approximation has a measurable error even for a smooth Gaussian process. At $q=1.1,\sigma=2.4$ m, its 1D mean is $5.469$ m, compared with the exact stationary crossing result $3.746$ m, an overestimate of $46.0\%$. At $q=5$ the error is $3.73\%$. This is why the paper compares data to finite threshold simulations as well as to limiting curves.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p271 sha256 803929ac82caf82979be0b2772f0e73098c8ff2e4a06e52f32333cfe00ef62f3 -->
p271, lines 271-271; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p269; next: p275

> **Reading the supplement carefully.** The printed supplement appears to contain algebraic errors in this calculation. Its Equation S18 prints multiplication by mean size where the preceding moment formula requires division. Its Equation S23 omits a factor $\sqrt2$ when solving the parabola in S22 for full width. In S25 the finite-height quadratic exponent should be negative. The derivation above retains the main paper's distributional form and obtains the constants from the displayed parabola, the moment integral, and the independent crossing calculation. These corrections concern the printed derivation; they do not establish that the simulation code or empirical fits used the same mistakes.[^p3-corrections]

### Why slices are a separate test

Location: line 273, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLICES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 275
Closing passage line: 283

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p275 sha256 f7a4d1df563695a6f456b6601656f1ad5be30d37c443f44aea6f10e8f88cc427 -->
p275, lines 275-275; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p271; next: p277

> A model can fit field volumes and still place activity incorrectly within those volumes. Slicing the map tests that internal spatial structure.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p277 sha256 7fcf2dfddf9b5536a5156f479958372adf7f807b8bf2cec9a245d5fda588393c -->
p277, lines 277-277; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p275; next: p279

> Restrict a Gaussian field to a straight line $x=x_0+tv$. At any chosen values $t_1,\ldots,t_n$, its values are a subset of the original jointly Gaussian values. They therefore remain jointly Gaussian. Along the line the covariance is $C((t-t')v)$; for a unit direction in an isotropic field it has the same local length scale $\sigma$. The same argument applies to a flat plane. First slicing and then thresholding gives the same active portions as thresholding and then slicing.[^p3-slices-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p279 sha256 92a306ecd5eec60105b9f9987ed8da5e18e423dd44d473ac381bb4733ac32c70 -->
p279, lines 279-279; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p277; next: p281

> Thus a line through a 2D or 3D map has the one dimensional excursion prediction, and a plane through a 3D map has the two dimensional prediction. The field being counted is now a connected active interval or patch in that slice. One 3D component can be missed, cut into multiple pieces, or intersect several slices.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p281 sha256 3715b83572476e5d4162bb74795b4c995d300529eeb05ffbd97764edfe1f3d55 -->
p281, lines 281-281; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p279; next: p283

> The sampling rule matters. Choosing one original field uniformly and cutting through its centre is different from choosing lines uniformly through space. Large fields are more likely to be hit by a spatially sampled line, and most hits pass away from their centres. The Gaussian restriction argument describes excursions along the sampled line. It does not justify assigning an unchanged field-counted radius distribution to all observed chords. In the high peak picture, an isotropic $d$ dimensional field has a radius of order $\sigma/q$; converting this same radius into $d$ dimensional size and then cutting it requires both hit probability and cut position.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p283 sha256 f9b37f82167d6d3b424c48741d7ee25c437a4f82c0a8ab256c7590156956fd22 -->
p283, lines 283-283; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p281; next: p287

> Read Figure 2 with these distinctions in mind. Panels A–B illustrate 2D thresholding. C measures lengths along slices, D measures original field areas, and E counts fields per observed cell. F–G show 3D examples; H, I, and J compare line lengths, planar areas, and full volumes. The dashed analytical curves change with the dimension of the measurement, even within one recorded map. The alternative spherical-field model in Figure S2 matches empirical volumes and peak rates by construction but fails these slice distributions and the topology test. Matching volumes alone is therefore insufficient.[^p3-slices-source]

### Why slopes at field boundaries are Rayleigh

Location: line 285, anchor `sec:PLACE-SLOPES`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 287
Closing passage line: 307

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p287 sha256 e344c1bebf0db137fc241848261e512767ee4d9a2a050e602e340fdef310b0a0 -->
p287, lines 287-287; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p283; next: p289

> The derivative at an arbitrary position is Gaussian. A field boundary is a selected position, however, and selecting it changes the derivative distribution.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p289 sha256 7dabf088a5cf0ce9cef482f3fea48c1c81ec2812167f551d752257cb6c5c15e1 -->
p289, lines 289-289; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p287; next: p297

> Return to the crossing integral and retain only slopes between $v$ and $v+dv$. Their contribution is proportional to $v p_{z'}(v)dv$. Dividing by the total contribution of positive slopes gives the slope density at a typical upcrossing:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p297 sha256 22f7985306579c6dae07453b788a543b49a4a30b7302abc69192d261fc39dc1c -->
p297, lines 297-297; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p289; next: p299

> The height density cancels because height and slope are independent. This Rayleigh law is therefore independent of $q$ under the smooth stationary Gaussian model, without a high threshold approximation. Absolute slopes at downward boundaries have the same law. The rate slope is $av$, so its scale also depends on the firing rate gain. This selection effect explains why a half Gaussian law for a positive derivative sampled uniformly in space would be wrong at crossings.[^p3-slope-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p299 sha256 65eb0b476fa28ef5ac949160ef950c255718a10344956fe5e81ac9a5dd55fc0a -->
p299, lines 299-299; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p297; next: p305

> There is also an independent check on the high threshold width constant. Starting at an upcrossing with slope $v$, the local excursion is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p305 sha256 8a1dc3346431d47dcdafec9e28737546824f0fb1d2eb9dfcbcabc9771538b7a2 -->
p305, lines 305-305; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p299; next: p307

> Its second root is $s=2\sigma^2v/q$. Changing variables in the exact crossing slope law gives $p(s)=2\beta s e^{-\beta s^2}$ with $\beta=q^2/(8\sigma^2)$, the same constant as the peak calculation for $v_1=2$.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p307 sha256 594efec82ac884dd9d09a6353fa67727a6b437fd2fe0866144d93a9a8f14f3c9 -->
p307, lines 307-307; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p305; next: p311

> Figure 3A identifies which width, peak, and slopes are measured. B compares local peak counts, C–D compare rate and size, and E compares boundary slopes. The paper measures boundary slopes by a finite difference between samples 0.1 m apart. This approximates a derivative and has its own spatial resolution. It is not a direct observation of the underlying synaptic input.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY -->

### Count holes as well as connected fields

Location: line 309, anchor `sec:PLACE-TOPOLOGY`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 311
Closing passage line: 390

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p311 sha256 31d510ecba43dd79dfb66dda81dae3efdb82e55e9e48675ffe3869e2af6d845e -->
p311, lines 311-311; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p307; next: p313

> Two maps can have the same field count and total active area but different holes. To test this part of the geometry, raise the activity threshold and watch how regions split, join, appear, and disappear.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p313 sha256 3d0d39a5c24c70f2abee8722b7fe93b9be259f41b051cd8275e2b6a39e20234f -->
p313, lines 313-313; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p311; next: p315

> The [**Euler characteristic**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}, written $\chi$, combines these counts. In 1D it equals the number of connected active intervals. In 2D it is the number of connected components minus the number of independent holes. Three disjoint disks have $\chi=3$; one connected ring has $\chi=1-1=0$. In 3D it is components minus independent tunnels plus enclosed cavities. A solid ball has $\chi=1$, a solid ring has $\chi=0$, and a hollow spherical shell has $\chi=2$. These counts are the first three [**Betti numbers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: numbers of independent components, loops, and cavities, respectively.[^p3-ec-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p315 sha256 c9ab6c8739082e3cca5422c92bf08d5d2051e86f32dfc0f9b6a9bea3168fa45f -->
p315, lines 315-315; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p313; next: p317

> The expected Euler characteristic has an exact Gaussian formula even where identifying it with field count would be wrong. We already derived the 1D case. To understand the higher dimensional factors, we use a mathematical input: for a smooth function on a compact domain whose critical points have zero gradient and invertible Hessians, the Euler characteristic of a level set region can be computed as a signed count of critical points, including boundary contributions. This is the relevant conclusion of Morse theory. We do not prove that topological theorem here. The calculation below shows how its Gaussian expectation produces the paper's polynomials and boundary terms; the general Gaussian result is given by Taylor's kinematic formula.[^p3-taylor]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p317 sha256 9d413cbb462a8805e6f97d941c734d4c3a1a0e7be354b3687908cb6fe05e8c05 -->
p317, lines 317-317; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p315; next: p325

> First take the interior of a $j$ dimensional region, with $j\le3$, and set $\lambda=\sigma^{-2}$. A critical point has gradient zero. The multidimensional Kac–Rice rule counts these zeros with the absolute Hessian determinant; the Morse signs replace that absolute determinant by $\det(-H)$. The resulting signed density above level $q$ is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p325 sha256 a54adb9dc66647dc11a47e1a99103408b5b1516b6a0959b97f26244ec1647232 -->
p325, lines 325-325; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p317; next: p327

> where $\phi(b)=e^{-b^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. The gradient's $j$ components are independent Gaussians of variance $\lambda$, so $p_{\nabla z}(0)=(2\pi\lambda)^{-j/2}$. The determinant measures how spatial volume near a gradient zero converts to gradient volume, just as the slope did for a scalar crossing.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p327 sha256 a43eb99f7072b08aea13f4bddee0b295009d95289a7d528c6b5cc3653298ebc9 -->
p327, lines 327-327; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p325; next: p335

> Here are the Gaussian algebra steps behind the determinant average. Write the conditional Hessian as $H=-b\lambda I+G$, where $G$ has zero mean. Differentiating an isotropic covariance four times gives, for distinct indices $i,k$,

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p335 sha256 611e89c52f18e27cbd5d4f4abe1873b2e7174ca8a18394cf1b65f67d44e707a4 -->
p335, lines 335-335; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p327; next: p341

> The constant $\kappa$ describes a fourth derivative of covariance. Conditioning on height subtracts $\lambda^2$ from the covariance of the two diagonal entries, but not from the off-diagonal variance. Hence each pair contributes

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p341 sha256 6eeec516802772ad652d966ac77d65a1e4ea637da1f35a5fe49eb8ff995f112f -->
p341, lines 341-341; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p335; next: p343

> For $j=1$, $\mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid b]=b\lambda$. For $j=2$, expand the two by two determinant to obtain $\lambda^2(b^2-1)$. For $j=3$, the leading term is $b^3\lambda^3$; the three index pairs each contribute $-b\lambda^3$, and all odd centered Gaussian products vanish. The result is $\lambda^3(b^3-3b)$. The unknown fourth derivative $\kappa$ has canceled.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p343 sha256 ccb3f22095106d1560d4d4abd636f04939cba9b69067374fb410037ad38bb37d -->
p343, lines 343-343; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p341; next: p353

> Integrating the three polynomials gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p353 sha256 e6a6ad7ece08367e18b645ac66fdea39bccdc022d9aad16e941f57eb9dfba4e0 -->
p353, lines 353-353; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p343; next: p361

> For example, differentiate $-q\phi(q)$ to get $(q^2-1)\phi(q)$, which verifies the second integral using its vanishing upper endpoint. Combining the determinant, gradient density, and integral yields

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p361 sha256 a7b5eaf9118abeb9aa49bb2332a925d2d12b0f8fadb10cec749495df1f5cc4a1 -->
p361, lines 361-361; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p353; next: p363

> These are signed densities per length, area, and volume, not three formulas for positive field counts.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p363 sha256 2c20f8c2c868fed491d30dc311931c8e5599abc6b9fa53e204f7213a6e3121d8 -->
p363, lines 363-363; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p361; next: p376

> Now include the boundary of an ordinary closed square or cube of side $L$, whose opposite faces are not joined and with every intersecting component retained. On a $j$ dimensional face, derivatives along the face give the same calculation as above. Each of its $d-j$ gradient components perpendicular to the face and directed out of the box must have the appropriate sign to contribute to the boundary Morse count. Symmetry and independence give a factor $2^{-(d-j)}$. There are $2^{d-j}\binom dj$ such faces, each of size $L^j$. Their combined coefficient is therefore $\binom djL^j$. Corners supply $Q(q)$ in total. With $r=L/\sigma$, the formulas become

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p376 sha256 326eb6657b7e2ac143f23713a457ffa350de85acbf14c87f48af0b7096e91aab -->
p376, lines 376-376; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p363; next: p378

> These reproduce the paper's Equations 8–10 for interval, square, and cube geometry. They are not the formulas for a periodic box. For a rectangle or rectangular room, replace $2L$ or $3L$ by the sum of side lengths, $3L^2$ by the sum of pairwise side products, and $L^3$ by volume. A general domain requires its own geometric boundary factors. Applying only the volume term to a small room would discard real boundary contributions.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p378 sha256 4a27ce931131f55588768a11c8f099c6588ee91b9bbe11870a389e6cbaec5605 -->
p378, lines 378-378; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p376; next: p380

> The limiting checks are informative. As $q\to-\infty$, the whole box becomes active and all three expectations tend to one. As $q\to+\infty$, they tend to zero. At intermediate thresholds the higher dimensional signed densities can be negative because tunnels or holes outnumber components. For example a cube with $L/\sigma=10$ has expected $\chi=-20.06$ at $q=0$. A negative expected Euler characteristic is meaningful; a negative expected field count would not be.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p380 sha256 b19a80efc6ef3cebb84771df7a93a897523e424ec20f0935d3698bb0248e429e -->
p380, lines 380-380; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p378; next: p388

> At a sufficiently high threshold, almost every component is a small solid blob with one peak and no holes. Then $\chi\simeq N$. For a large box, retaining the leading volume term and the leading power of $q$ gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p388 sha256 5eee88f6ac607536212e2dbb3e3b9df22f068e713fb3e6dcf608eb26d6fd3b62 -->
p388, lines 388-388; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p380; next: p390

> where $V$ is environment length, area, or volume. Dividing $VQ(q)$ by this count and using $Q(q)\simeq\phi(q)/q$ recovers $\mu_s\simeq(\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma/q)^d$, independently checking the size-law mean. The exact EC formula and this approximate component count have different ranges of validity.[^p3-higher-count]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p390 sha256 378192a1076eb211507796aa3a77b10ceff0b27946bf47939d54b587c90b9f7b -->
p390, lines 390-390; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p388; next: p394

> Read Figure 3F–H as whole curves, rather than single field counts. Increasing the applied firing rate cutoff by $t\ge0$ changes the normalized level to $q+t/a$. It can reveal separation between peaks or remove components. The measured maps only test levels at or above their original effective cutoff; the rest of an analytic curve is a prediction. The gain was matched using mean maximum firing rate before the EC comparison, so the horizontal conversion from Hz to normalized level also uses fitted information. Two dimensional holes and three dimensional tunnels help explain the nonmonotonic curves.[^p3-ec-source]

Claims to check:

- Claim c1, line 313: "Three disjoint disks have $\chi=3$; one connected ring has $\chi=1-1=0$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c2, line 313: "A solid ball has $\chi=1$, a solid ring has $\chi=0$, and a hollow spherical shell has $\chi=2$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c3, line 313: "These counts are the first three [**Betti numbers**]{.definition-term custom-style="Definition Term"}: numbers of independent components, loops, and cavities, respectively.[^p3-ec-source]" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c4, line 315: "The calculation below shows how its Gaussian expectation produces the paper's polynomials and boundary terms; the general Gaussian result is given by Taylor's kinematic formula.[^p3-taylor]" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c5, line 317: "First take the interior of a $j$ dimensional region, with $j\le3$, and set $\lambda=\sigma^{-2}$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c6, line 319: "$$ \eta_j(q)=p_{\nabla z}(0) \int_q^\infty \mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid z=b,\nabla z=0]\,\phi(b)\,db, $$" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c7, line 325: "where $\phi(b)=e^{-b^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c8, line 325: "The gradient's $j$ components are independent Gaussians of variance $\lambda$, so $p_{\nabla z}(0)=(2\pi\lambda)^{-j/2}$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c9, line 341: "For $j=1$, $\mathbb E[\det(-H)\mid b]=b\lambda$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c10, line 341: "For $j=2$, expand the two by two determinant to obtain $\lambda^2(b^2-1)$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c11, line 341: "For $j=3$, the leading term is $b^3\lambda^3$; the three index pairs each contribute $-b\lambda^3$, and all odd centered Gaussian products vanish." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c12, line 341: "The result is $\lambda^3(b^3-3b)$." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c13, line 345: "$$ \begin{aligned} \int_q^\infty b\phi(b)\,db&=\phi(q),\\ \int_q^\infty(b^2-1)\phi(b)\,db&=q\phi(q),\\ \int_q^\infty(b^3-3b)\phi(b)\,db&=(q^2-1)\phi(q). \end{aligned} $$" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c14, line 353: "For example, differentiate $-q\phi(q)$ to get $(q^2-1)\phi(q)$, which verifies the second integral using its vanishing upper endpoint." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c15, line 355: "$$ \eta_1=\frac{e^{-q^2/2}}{2\pi\sigma},\qquad \eta_2=\frac{q e^{-q^2/2}}{(2\pi)^{3/2}\sigma^2},\qquad \eta_3=\frac{(q^2-1)e^{-q^2/2}}{(2\pi)^2\sigma^3}. $$" -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]
- Claim c16, line 363: "Now include the boundary of an ordinary closed square or cube of side $L$, whose opposite faces are not joined and with every intersecting component retained." -> [verdict: true as written, condition stated, fixed, or not a claim; checked: how]

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/05-place-fields.md::sec:PLACE-DATA -->

### What was fitted and what was tested

Location: line 392, anchor `sec:PLACE-DATA`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 394
Closing passage line: 419

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p394 sha256 fcc7333ac30c95d0624d811447fbe4a23eb853252323fcd377d119206443ec5b -->
p394, lines 394-394; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p390; next: p396

> The experiments supply estimated firing rate maps, not the hidden Gaussian input. To assess a prediction, we must therefore track both which summaries set its parameters and which processing decisions change the map's geometry.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p396 sha256 6fc88a9a6f9d269e8bf8bdf952dd12345dcd732a864a643ac2dbf710f2953bc1 -->
p396, lines 396-396; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p394; next: p407

> The paper reanalyzes earlier bat and rodent recordings. It uses different available summaries in different conditions. The inspected article and supplement do not clearly give the final included cell count for every analysis, particularly the long-tunnel and 3D reanalyses; counts from an original recording study should therefore not be silently substituted for those final samples.[^p3-processing]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p407 sha256 cf08531dee7b08e4bf8a41ab1a4f001748c1e62c06748bde0ed65a0690be08a8 -->
p407, lines 407-407; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p396; next: p409

> The STAR Methods call the virtual-track animals rats in one sentence; Figure 1F and the main results identify them as mice. Several STAR figure cross-references also appear displaced. The table follows the main figure captions and the analyses described in full, rather than those isolated labels.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p409 sha256 f52adbb36eaf378ad69f55739a6c40c4b35365dbc942de868f7c12a2bc37c833 -->
p409, lines 409-409; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p407; next: p411

> Figure 1C distinguishes a limiting analytical Rayleigh curve from the finite threshold simulation histogram. Its parameters were constrained by both mean length and mean gap, so the full length histogram is an additional check. Figure 1D does the same for gaps. E tests skew and excess kurtosis of log lengths. For $Y=\log s$, write $m_k=\mathbb E[(Y-\mathbb EY)^k]$; then skew is $m_3/m_2^{3/2}$ and excess kurtosis is $m_4/m_2^2-3$. A lognormal size distribution makes $Y$ Gaussian and both population quantities zero. Finite sample estimates still fluctuate, which is why the figure compares simulated sampling distributions.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p411 sha256 e0ff8f69b6e4e7b7cc04aa3cd2ba415f0e2f0172ac511dbd6a34b2ad24437307 -->
p411, lines 411-411; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p409; next: p413

> For the long-tunnel data, the authors compared the Gaussian excursion model with a fitted lognormal using likelihoods and criteria that penalize fitted parameter count. Those comparisons favored the Gaussian model. These are in-sample model comparisons. They are not reported held-out forecasts. Model histogram error bars come from $10^4$ simulations matched to the number of recorded cells; they do not quantify every source of animal-level or measurement uncertainty.[^p3-fit-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p413 sha256 945a2125a65191b34df6580b6cf615bd02b9377dc3601cce5f6233555276094b -->
p413, lines 413-413; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p411; next: p415

> The processing choices affect the quantities being compared. Bat maps were rectified at about 0.5 Hz for denoising. The 3D maps, sampled in 10 cm bins, also received a median filter. Fields larger than three standard deviations above mean 3D volume were removed as possible merges, including from slice analyses. Peak counting excluded incomplete boundary fields and used a range of smoothing widths. The 1D rate versus width comparison used an additional 1 Hz cutoff. These decisions can remove small components, alter peaks, and change tails. They belong to the definition of the analyzed fields.[^p3-processing]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p415 sha256 40d198f32171847c66abe0fa5dca8623a424e859692b7918f5c2d43ce1fd80ea -->
p415, lines 415-415; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p413; next: p417

> The supplement adds useful checks. Figure S1 matches the rat maze's 15 cm minimum length cutoff in simulations and removes unusually large empirical fields; changing that cutoff changes log-length moments. Figure S5 shows sensitivity of the higher-moment comparison across small denoising thresholds, while the rate versus width slope becomes stable above about 1 Hz. Figure S2 compares the spherical-field alternative and measures boundary curvature. Locally, mean curvature averages bending in two principal directions and Gaussian curvature multiplies them: a spherical patch bends the same way in both directions, while a saddle bends in opposite directions. These are extra shape measurements; they are not needed to derive the field-size law. Surface representations and finite differences affect their estimates.[^p3-supp-tests]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p417 sha256 f3ab73148350b61d76a33097435dc578a9f1ca338b1d4d18ec526535397d61ca -->
p417, lines 417-417; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p415; next: p419

> Figure 4A–B summarizes fitted parameter changes across a small set of environments. The correlation length increases with environment size while the threshold generally decreases. Increasing $\sigma$ reduces crossings per metre; decreasing $q$ increases them. Both parameters also affect mean length, so these effects must be considered together. Figure S3 reports count scaling exponents below one and hence a weak decline in count per unit environment size. These cross-experiment trends do not establish a biological scaling law. In particular, the 3D table's reported $\sigma=4.0$ has an unclear distance-unit conversion relative to the spatial binning, so it should not be read as a verified value in metres.[^p3-trends]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p419 sha256 b55c4804307932ad51ca9f7cae0073f9ba356aad17cd6b5853035339596dd39c -->
p419, lines 419-419; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p417; next: p423

> The model also allows differences between cells. Figure S4 draws a positive threshold separately for each cell and keeps that threshold for two otherwise independent maps representing opposite flight directions. Low-threshold cells tend to have more and larger fields in both maps; this shared cell property creates correlations between flight directions even when field locations differ between directions. Two distribution parameters were chosen to match the two empirical correlations, so those correlations are fitted features of this extension. Its continued agreement with length and gap distributions is the additional comparison. This extension should not be described as a two-parameter model shared by all cells.[^p3-threshold-variation]

### What Gaussian maps imply about synaptic input

Location: line 421, anchor `sec:PLACE-WIRING`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 423
Closing passage line: 462

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p423 sha256 30e3871f3bad882f606126c235a5966435172ad279aeb11adc8329e5945ee265 -->
p423, lines 423-423; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p419; next: p425

> The statistical model becomes biologically useful if it suggests how a circuit could make the maps. A random weighted sum of many inputs is one possible mechanism, and the supplement makes its assumptions explicit.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p425 sha256 d4754d9c681c5a9275cb92565a055d40d544fb8d2dfc5be32d26301a27b68779 -->
p425, lines 425-425; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p423; next: p431

> Let $u_i(x)$ be the fixed spatial tuning of input neuron $i$, and let independent weights $W_i$ have zero mean and variance $v_W$. The profiles $u_i$ may have nonzero spatial means. The expectation below is over weights: their zero means make the summed input $h(x)$ have mean zero. The postsynaptic input is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p431 sha256 bd69187085e59bca3bf71c3f09586e171956015e2201fa49b5c5586309f76996 -->
p431, lines 431-431; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p425; next: p433

> At a finite list of positions this is a sum of independent random vectors, one per presynaptic cell. Under a multivariate central limit condition, with a suitable normalization and no single input dominating, the sum approaches a Gaussian vector as the number of contributing inputs grows. Applying this at every finite list supplies the Gaussian-process description. The theorem needs many contributing inputs at the positions in question; a large anatomical input count alone does not show that this condition holds.[^p3-wiring-source]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p433 sha256 d2a002d6434381ff1dc22fa84811a52d1e42a1ec251760afaadf1cda16ded894 -->
p433, lines 433-433; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p431; next: p443

> The covariance calculation exposes which assumption supplies spatial structure:

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p443 sha256 597d32eb83bc21fe4c797636463d0e5fd28d6b21905a2fad133f199afeef9d36 -->
p443, lines 443-443; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p433; next: p449

> If inputs are translated copies $u_i(x)=u_0(x-x_i)$ with centres densely and uniformly covering space at density $\rho$, the sum is approximately

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p449 sha256 0f9898b3ae37aa6f69a5bdebf4f1e28211db5ac5e19288b94e63092a61132d34 -->
p449, lines 449-449; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p443; next: p451

> Uniform coverage supplies stationarity away from boundaries; smooth input tuning supplies smoothness. Random weights supply the Gaussian limit under their independence and size conditions. These are separate assumptions. For heterogeneous shapes, average the last integral over the distribution of input shapes, as in Methods S2, Equation S8.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p451 sha256 5ec0a727a94019dc93bbc6230121b363e41d23c719b1332f15161be79971b08a -->
p451, lines 451-451; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p449; next: p458

> For example, take $u_0(s)=R_0e^{-s^2/(2b^2)}$ in one dimension. Completing the square gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p458 sha256 1cdf2eb9d5d87b61ffcc8eedd750fe75ba8241962d861fcf9a564a23883b86a0 -->
p458, lines 458-458; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p451; next: p460

> The covariance's Gaussian standard deviation is $\sqrt2 b$. This calculation specifies the width convention, avoiding an ambiguous assertion that covariance is simply “twice as wide.”

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p460 sha256 e835b9977f7aed2f3e810f609834c0207090ed4589a1870f20eacd948c6210d8 -->
p460, lines 460-460; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p458; next: p462

> Agreement with the Gaussian field predictions supports this random-summation account as a possible explanation. It does not uniquely recover synaptic weights from rate maps. Correlated inputs, a structured component added to many random contributions, plasticity, or recurrent processing could also produce approximately Gaussian spatial input under some conditions. The paper measures rate maps, rather than the relevant weights or subthreshold input directly. Its analyzed environments were familiar and fairly uniform; stationarity may fail near landmarks, boundaries, rewards, or in changing tasks.[^p3-discussion]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p462 sha256 3938564b29656cee9c9faf1eee89fa9cba0fa180eef7a7079df6e737ca1fd7e5 -->
p462, lines 462-462; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p460; next: none

> The result to carry into the synthesis is therefore specific: thresholding a smooth spatial Gaussian field links field arrangement, shape, and topology through a small set of parameters and gives several successful cross-checks. Turning that statistical agreement into a claim about circuit mechanism requires additional measurements or perturbations.

## ch:SYNTHESIS - Relating spatial codes, memory, and behavior

Source file: `content/chapters/06-synthesis.md`

Purpose: A model can explain the shape of a neural code without explaining how that code is maintained. It can also explain fluctuations inside a circuit without uniquely locating the source of a measured behavior. The three papers become more useful together when these different claims are kept attached to their actual mathematical and experimental tests.

Prerequisites: ch:EYE-DRIFT, ch:PLACE-FIELDS

Sidecar sections: Three questions about one represented quantity, How a static map can carry a moving estimate, Example: opposite rate changes from the same position error, What makes an experimental test restrictive, Related work and what it changes, A joint comparison that remains to be done

Blocks: 0 figure(s), 0 equation(s), 0 code block(s)

Result labels: No Answer/Conclusion/Feedback label detected

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-QUESTIONS -->

### Three questions about one represented quantity

Location: line 5, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-QUESTIONS`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 7
Closing passage line: 17

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p7 sha256 d59a52e9861f261353d78533cc7d141cb16e8c7c2d1a217cf82e734c79e6f585 -->
p7, lines 7-7; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p3; next: p9

> For a circuit representing position, three questions arise. What activity pattern corresponds to each position? How does the represented position evolve when there is no corrective input? How does a downstream physical system transform that changing signal into an observable movement? The place-field model addresses the first question. The persistent-activity model addresses the second. The eye-drift model combines a candidate answer to the second with a detailed answer to the third.[^s-three]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p9 sha256 5f0d743f2d1f49e3a96956d00be8a1bf6f9a70134b740a7f87c145add2891d5f -->
p9, lines 9-9; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p7; next: p17

> The objects differ even when the word position appears in all three. In 2012, $\theta(t)$ locates a network state on its attractor. In 2022, the internal eye-position command and actual eye angle are separate quantities linked through motor neurons and mechanics. In 2025, $x$ locates an animal in an environment, and $h_i(x)$ is a spatial input function assigned to a cell. The Gaussian process does not update a remembered $x$ through time.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p17 sha256 ad2dbfd83035c66765e571fea07591056a20766e6319089852dd996bc7f4dc5a -->
p17, lines 17-17; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p9; next: p21

> The 2012 and 2022 connection is direct: neural noise can move a maintained eye-position command, which then drives muscles. The 2022 result provides evidence for a substantial shared source before the final motor neurons and shows that integrator diffusion is sufficient within the constructed model. It does not measure the circuit's Fisher information or test equality in the 2012 bound. The 2025 connection is broader: an internally coherent position estimate can be expressed through spatially irregular tuning curves.

> Suppose a network maintains a one-dimensional estimate $x(t)$ and cell $i$ has a fixed expected-rate function $f_i(x)$. Its time-dependent expected rate is

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p27 sha256 52960562ed8a66c6287ff261c2b2e31a70e029b0a57df378e7696dd4eae09fec -->
p27, lines 27-27; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p21; next: p29

> Here $f_i$ describes spatial tuning and $x(t)$ describes dynamics. A source of time variation has entered even though $f_i$ is fixed. Actual spike counts add another layer of variability around the expected rate. This construction is a guide calculation connecting the papers' objects, not a model tested jointly by the three studies.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p29 sha256 26ec0a2ba2dd9d70b8a18d86e0a17bc2c5aa95313651452ae8db960664f8c046 -->
p29, lines 29-29; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p27; next: p36

> Consider a short interval of duration $t$ starting at $x_0$. Assume that the estimate's local displacement $\delta x=x(t)-x_0$ has mean zero and variance $2Dt$, and that it is small enough to linearize each rate curve. Taylor expansion gives

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p36 sha256 84605c14ed46c29ad7def1fcb1f9ae4615ec24f004367b7e54f6a6a7710a93e4 -->
p36, lines 36-36; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p29; next: p44

> To leading order, the change is $\delta R_i=f_i'(x_0)\delta x$. Multiplying the centered changes for neurons $i$ and $j$ and averaging yields

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p44 sha256 999290bb61c28849dadda74211bbbac06315dce9f77c1e44aab3636d71d726ea -->
p44, lines 44-44; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p36; next: p48

> This covariance concerns expected-rate changes caused by the common represented displacement, conditional on the starting position. It is not a formula for every covariance in a spiking recurrent network. It neglects higher-order changes in tuning and additional private or shared fluctuations. Near a threshold or over a large spatial displacement, the linear approximation must be revisited.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p48 sha256 f96cb6dfc04493e98b98a5974085326ceac39a611e1a2675b4a9ba715be224f9 -->
p48, lines 48-48; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p44; next: p61

> Let the local slopes of two rate curves be $f_1'=2$ Hz/m and $f_2'=-1$ Hz/m. Suppose $D=0.01$ m²/s and the interval is 0.5 s. Then the variance of the common position displacement is $2Dt=0.01$ m². The contributions to rate variance and covariance are

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p61 sha256 48433ea57f29595c3bc120cb3f5db4a6e290fcf35a3e630b5974341a5d7cd7fd -->
p61, lines 61-61; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p48; next: p63

> With only this one source, the two rate changes have correlation $-1$: one rises whenever the other falls. Adding independent private noise lowers the magnitude. The negative covariance here comes from opposite tuning slopes under a common position change. It does not establish an inhibitory connection between the cells.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p63 sha256 f85833f7c39c1eb1c221033ea2363710150a1a17bd0206fc74565fa5a4b823a3 -->

> This example explains why irregular or mixed single-cell responses can coexist with a low-dimensional population fluctuation. It also shows the extra evidence required to connect a spatial map to a dynamical mechanism: one must measure how population changes align with the directions implied by those maps, rather than infer dynamics from field shapes alone.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-EVIDENCE -->

### What makes an experimental test restrictive

Location: line 65, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-EVIDENCE`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 67
Closing passage line: 71

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p67 sha256 5afaa100f7556f87ebae41ae0b3587ccb817daedee5011c8cf81572c7de33f71 -->
p67, lines 67-67; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p63; next: p69

> A model that matches one distribution may have enough adjustable parameters to do so for several different reasons. The place-field paper gains support by fitting arrangement parameters and then comparing additional features, including slices and shape statistics. The separate firing-rate multiplier used in some comparisons should remain visible when counting how many quantities were predicted without further fitting. A successful high-threshold formula is also a statement about its limiting assumptions; finite-threshold simulations and the original recordings provide different kinds of checks.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p69 sha256 55cdfdfc2ed1163d3f32b6fb96fc7838f8e4c3ae9b27c57af4810e41ddd29bfd -->
p69, lines 69-69; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p67; next: p71

> The eye paper uses a different strategy. It calibrates a movement-to-firing relationship, inverts it, and compares the resulting single-neuron displacement predictions with measured displacement. Shared covariance then carries information that independent private fluctuations cannot supply under the decomposition's assumptions. The additional MSD comparison tests the time structure: increasing stationary motor-noise amplitude cannot by itself reproduce the accumulating upstream signal. Calibration validity, source correlations, two animals, and horizontal movement remain part of the result's scope.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p71 sha256 2006283d69a6f9959b971d4cab5c32ca6d393130f83f0799af634603ba33b977 -->
p71, lines 71-71; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p69; next: p75

> The 2012 inequality is a mathematical constraint rather than an experimental fit. Its derivation specifies what noise and dynamics it covers. An experimental comparison would need estimates of diffusion, the relevant time constants, and the appropriate instantaneous information rate, all under compatible conditions. Agreement with $1/N$ scaling alone would not test the entire inequality, since several mechanisms can share that scaling.

<!-- pedagogy-unit: content/chapters/06-synthesis.md::sec:SYNTHESIS-RELATED -->

### Related work and what it changes

Location: line 73, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-RELATED`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 75
Closing passage line: 83

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p75 sha256 5dec496c98146823350b09881ccab70d2fa6b33ed33a808c29e5d90565ea82f4 -->
p75, lines 75-75; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p71; next: p77

> The foundational eye-position account is Seung's line-attractor model. Aksay and colleagues then used recordings and perturbations in goldfish to constrain integrator circuitry. These establish the dynamical problem and experimental architecture behind the 2022 model. Neither supplies the macaque displacement-variance estimate. Burak and Fiete's earlier grid-cell study puts a related integrator problem in navigation: recurrent population activity is driven by velocity and accumulates path-integration errors. Its accuracy ranges belong to the simulated networks and parameter choices, rather than all biological grid circuits.[^s-attractors]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p77 sha256 a3896815b5775509a91561f39d93c643a7340b3c85cf1daf7f7d6ada3ef8ec43 -->
p77, lines 77-77; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p75; next: p79

> Noise-driven storage also connects to memory capacity. Koyluoglu and colleagues ask how noise limits recall as the delay and number of stored items increase. Their comparison with human data shows why direct storage of several independent values need not capture observed behavior; transformations before and after storage alter the performance prediction. Seeholzer and colleagues examine how short-term changes in synaptic strength affect diffusion and systematic drift. These papers change the storage assumptions rather than invalidate the 2012 calculation inside its original model.[^s-extensions]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p79 sha256 f8a7a38c667323fc250351c8ac134af9dfb2ae17f5c1a584b5afaf560c2f464d -->
p79, lines 79-79; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p77; next: p81

> For place fields, Eliav and colleagues' long-tunnel recordings and Harland and colleagues' large-arena recordings supply the multiple-field, multiple-scale observations. Yartsev and Ulanovsky supply the three-dimensional bat recordings. The 2025 paper reanalyzes those existing data, so its agreement across dimensions is not three newly collected experiments under one protocol. The later study by Maimon and colleagues directly compares CA3 and CA1 in large tunnels: predominantly single fields in CA3 contrast with multiple fields in CA1, while field sizes are similar. That distinction supports treating the CA1 statistics and the upstream spatial circuit as related but separately constrained parts of the explanation.[^s-place-data]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p81 sha256 efd08ca83cfd07253e582947ff743358bc039972f97bf560f543dd4c5f7e1d75 -->
p81, lines 81-81; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p79; next: p83

> There are also direct reasons to avoid reading Gaussian spatial statistics as a unique wiring diagram. Druckmann and colleagues report structured synaptic connectivity between hippocampal regions. Bittner and colleagues show how electrical events in dendrites, the branching input structures of neurons, can induce changes in synaptic strength and form CA1 place fields. Structure and learning can therefore be present even when a random-function model describes many aggregate field statistics. The model's success leaves open which circuit mechanism produces these maps. Input, connectivity, learning, or perturbation measurements could distinguish the possibilities.[^s-wiring]

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p83 sha256 0fe304ba8f31463bd41ac7aa64ce7c4ccea4d5ac4ea355cd092522c5714ce409 -->
p83, lines 83-83; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p81; next: p87

> Finally, the source and use of eye motion are different questions. Nghiem and colleagues combine human eye tracking with a model of how eye movement affects visual signals to explain why an intermediate amount of fixational motion can help fine visual discrimination. A movement that partly originates from internal noise can still have useful sensory consequences, and feedback can regulate its statistics. Benefit for perception does not uniquely identify the neural generator.[^s-acuity]


Claim-bearing content: none

### A joint comparison that remains to be done

Location: line 85, anchor `sec:SYNTHESIS-NEXT`

First content kind: `prose`

Opening passage line: 87
Closing passage line: 89

Paragraphs in source order (complete `PARAGRAPH_REVIEW.json`):

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p87 sha256 554a56c2e1248d82c8e4ab9880c66883c528e1c8d1aee75e6936cc2b7734ca4f -->
p87, lines 87-87; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p83; next: p89

> The combined calculation suggests a concrete research direction. For the same population, first estimate the tuning maps with their uncertainty. Then measure fluctuations during periods where external anchoring is weak and test whether their covariance is aligned with changes along those maps. Fit diffusion only over a range where the local approximation and measurement corrections are justified. A downstream behavioral comparison must additionally include the physical or neural filters between representation and measurement.

<!-- pedagogy-paragraph: p89 sha256 c537125369470a7f35ab03d1d20ae2e553c43afbd9711807edfadd01ab38db2e -->
p89, lines 89-89; prose; priority: unchanged; previous: p87; next: none

> This would ask whether one system satisfies both spatial-statistics and dynamical-stability constraints. A static Gaussian-process fit alone cannot supply the diffusion coefficient. A diffusion fit alone cannot establish the distribution of fields. A joint success would be more restrictive, while a mismatch would point to a particular assumption to change: spatial input statistics, recurrent maintenance, the observation filter, or their coupling. This is proposed follow-up work, not a replication claimed by this guide.
